Hadronic light-by-light scattering contribution to the muon g − 2 g-2 from lattice QCD: semi-analytical calculation of the QED kernel Preprint: MITP-22-083 DESY-22-163
Nils Asmussen, En-Hung Chao, Antoine Gérardin, Jeremy R. Green, Renwick J. Hudspith, Harvey B. Meyer, Andreas Nyffeler

Abstract
Hadronic light-by-light scattering is one of the virtual processes that causes the gyromagnetic factor g g of the muon to deviate from the value of two predicted by Dirac’s theory. This process makes one of the largest contributions to the uncertainty of the Standard Model prediction for the muon ( g − 2 ) (g-2) . Lattice QCD allows for a first-principles approach to computing this non-perturbative effect. In order to avoid power-law finite-size artifacts generated by virtual photons in lattice simulations, we follow a coordinate-space approach involving a weighted integral over the vertices of the QCD four-point function of the electromagnetic current carried by the quarks. Here we present in detail the semi-analytical calculation of the QED part of the amplitude, employing position-space perturbation theory in continuous, infinite four-dimensional Euclidean space. We also provide some useful information about a computer code for the numerical implementation of our approach that has been made public at https://github.com/RJHudspith/KQED .



August 9, 2026

I
Introduction

The anomalous magnetic moment of the muon,
a_{\mu}\equiv(g-2)_{\mu}/2
,
characterizes its response to a magnetic field, and is one of the most
precisely known quantities in fundamental physics. Currently, the
experimental world average
Muong-2:2021ojo
;
Muong-2:2006rrc
is
in tension with the theoretical evaluation based on the Standard Model
(SM) of particle physics. On the basis of the Muon
g-2
Theory Initiative’s 2020 White Paper
(WP)
Aoyama:2020ynm
with input from Refs.​
Aoyama:2012wk
;
Aoyama:2019ryr
;
Czarnecki:2002nt
;
Gnendiger:2013pva
;
Davier:2017zfy
;
Keshavarzi:2018mgv
;
Colangelo:2018mtw
;
Hoferichter:2019mqg
;
Davier:2019can
;
Keshavarzi:2019abf
;
Kurz:2014wya
;
Melnikov:2003xd
;
Masjuan:2017tvw
;
Colangelo:2017fiz
;
Hoferichter:2018kwz
;
Gerardin:2019vio
;
Bijnens:2019ghy
;
Colangelo:2019uex
;
Blum:2019ugy
;
Colangelo:2014qya
,
the tension is at the
4.2\sigma
level. Theoretical and experimental uncertainties are practically
equal and just under the level of 0.4 ppm. While a tension between
theory and experiment has persisted for about twenty years,
the 2021 result of the Fermilab Muon
(g-2)
experiment
Muong-2:2021ojo
has increased this tension
and thereby revived the general interest in possible explanations involving
beyond-the-Standard-Model physics, see e.g. ​
Athron:2021iuf
.

The leading prediction for
a_{\mu}
in QED is

\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}

Schwinger:1948iu
, where
\alpha
is the
fine-structure constant. Effects of the strong interaction enter
at O(
\alpha^{2}
). Due to the low mass scale of the muon,
strong-interaction effects in the muon
(g-2)
must be treated in
their full, non-perturbative complexity. As a result, the theory
uncertainty of this precision observable is entirely dominated by the
hadronic contributions.

The leading hadronic contribution goes under the name of hadronic
vacuum polarization (HVP). The situation around the muon
(g-2)
has
become more intricate with the publication of a lattice-QCD based
calculation
Borsanyi:2020mff
of the HVP contribution, which
finds a larger value than the dispersion-theory based estimate of the
WP and would bring the overall theory prediction into far better
agreement with the experimental value of
a_{\mu}
. Thus it will be
crucial to resolve the tension between the different determinations of
the HVP contribution in order to capitalize on the
expected improvements in the experimental determinations of
a_{\mu}
: a
reduction by more than a factor of two is expected from the Fermilab
Muon
g-2
experiment
Muong-2:2015xgu
, and further measurements
are planned at J-PARC
Abe:2019thb
and considered at
PSI
Aiba:2021bxe
.

An O(
\alpha^{3}
) hadronic contribution to
a_{\mu}
, known as the
hadronic light-by-light (HLbL) contribution, also adds
significantly to the error budget of the SM prediction.
It can be represented as the Feynman diagram depicted in Fig.
1
.
In the WP error budget for
a_{\mu}
, its assigned uncertainty is
0.15 ppm. Therefore, anticipating error reductions in the HVP
contribution and in the experimental measurements, it is crucial to
further reduce the uncertainty on the HLbL contribution by at least a factor of two.

The HLbL contribution is conceptually more complex than the HVP
contribution.
On the other hand, being suppressed by an additional
power of the fine-structure constant
\alpha
, the requirements on its
relative precision are far less stringent: the uncertainty quoted in
the WP corresponds to 20%. In recent years, the HLbL contribution has been evaluated
using either dispersive methods,
for which a full result can be found in the WP
Aoyama:2020ynm

based on Refs.​
Melnikov:2003xd
;
Masjuan:2017tvw
;
Colangelo:2017fiz
;
Hoferichter:2018kwz
;
Gerardin:2019vio
;
Bijnens:2019ghy
;
Colangelo:2019uex
;
Pauk:2014rta
;
Danilkin:2016hnh
;
Jegerlehner:2017gek
;
Knecht:2018sci
;
Eichmann:2019bqf
;
Roig:2019reh
;
Colangelo:2014qya
,
or lattice QCD (
Blum:2019ugy
and
Chao:2021tvp
–
Chao:2022xzg
). Good agreement is found among
the three evaluations within the quoted uncertainties.

The purpose of the present paper is to provide a detailed account of
the computational strategy underlying our recent
calculation
Chao:2021tvp
;
Chao:2022xzg
. Its full development spanned several
years, with progress reported in a number of conferences since
2015
Talk_Asmussen_DPG_2015
;
Green:2015mva
;
Asmussen:2016lse
;
Asmussen:2017bup
;
Asmussen:2018ovy
;
Asmussen:2019act
.
The basic idea is to treat the muon and photon propagators of Fig.
1

in position-space perturbation theory, in the continuum and
in infinite-volume, while the ‘hadronic blob’ is to be treated in
lattice QCD on a spatial torus. Thus much of this paper is concerned
with the semi-analytical calculation of the QED part of the amplitude.

The idea to compute the HLbL contribution to
a_{\mu}
was first
proposed in 2005
Hayakawa:2005eq
, with a follow-up three years
later
Chowdhury:2008zz
. These initial methods finally led to
the 2014 publication
Blum:2014oka
. In parallel to the
development of our strategy, the RBC/UKQCD collaboration then also
worked on improving its computational methods
Blum:2015gfa
,
with a first exploratory calculation at physical quark masses
published in
Blum:2016lnc
. These methods are based on treating
the QED parts of Fig.
1
within the lattice field
theory set up on a finite torus. Starting with
Ref. ​
Blum:2017cer
, the RBC/UKQCD collaboration also developed
its own tools to treat the muon and photon propagators in infinite
volume. We will return in section
VIII.1
to some aspects
of the cross-fertilization that occurred between the two groups.

It is also worth pointing out other, less direct approaches that have
been pursued towards better determining the HLbL
contribution to the muon
(g-2)
using lattice QCD. Of all meson
exchanges, the neutral-pion pole contribution is by far the largest,
and we have published two lattice calculations of its transition form
factor describing its coupling to two (in general) virtual
photons
Gerardin:2016cqj
;
Gerardin:2019vio
. Since the
\pi^{0}

contribution is the numerically dominant one at long distances, having
a dedicated determination thereof also helps control systematic errors
at long distances in the direct lattice calculation
Chao:2020kwq

based on the formalism presented in this paper. As a separate
line of study, we have investigated the HLbL
scattering amplitude at Euclidean kinematics
Green:2015sra
,
particularly its eight independent forward-scattering components,
which depend on three invariant kinematic variables. Knowing these
amplitudes allows one to constrain the contributions of various meson
exchanges
Green:2015sra
;
Gerardin:2017ryf
by parametrizing their transition
form factors, information which may subsequently be used to estimate
the HLbL contribution to the muon
(g-2)
.

This manuscript is organized as follows. Section
II

presents the general features of our position-space approach and the
master-formula for
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
. The ingredients necessary for
the evaluation of the ‘QED kernel’ describing all purely QED elements
of the amplitude depicted in Fig.
1
are collected
in section
III
, at the end of which the averaging over the direction
of the muon momentum is performed. A relatively straightforward method of
evaluating the final convolution integral yielding
the weight functions parametrizing the QED kernel is
described in section
IV
. An alternative, ultimately
favored method based on the multipole expansion of the photon
propagator in Gegenbauer polynomials is presented in
section
V
. Some technical aspects of
the numerical implementation are given in
section
VI
. Then several models are
used in section
VII
to compute various contributions to
the four-point function of the electromagnetic current in QED and
QCD. Since these contributions to the muon
(g-2)
have been computed
previously (using analytical methods in momentum-space), we use them
to perform tests of our position-space QED kernel in section

VIII
. Published results obtained
in lattice QCD by the present methods for the quark-connected contribution
are also reviewed in that section. Section
IX
collects
our concluding remarks. The appendices contain additional material
useful for numerical implementations, providing in particular the
kernel asymptotics for various special kinematic regimes.
The final appendix (
E
) provides some
information about a computer code available for the numerical implementation
of our approach based on the results of section
V
.

II
Master formula for
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
in position space

We are interested in the hadronic light-by-light (HLbL) scattering
contribution to the anomalous magnetic moment of the muon, see
Fig.
1
.

Figure 1:
Hadronic light-by-light scattering diagram in the muon

(g-2)
.

The basic idea of our approach is to treat the four-point function of
hadronic electromagnetic currents, represented by the blob in
Fig.
1
, in lattice QCD regularization, while for the
remaining QED part with photons and muons, we use continuum, Euclidean
position-space perturbation theory in infinite
volume
Talk_Asmussen_DPG_2015
;
Green:2015mva
;
Asmussen:2016lse
. As we will show, this allows a Lorentz covariant,
semi-analytical calculation of the QED part which avoids power-law
finite-volume corrections
1/L^{2}
in
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
due to the
massless photons. An approach in position space is most natural, since
in lattice QCD one obtains the four-point correlation function
directly in position space. Furthermore, it will be possible to get
directly the HLbL contribution
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
as a spatial moment
of the four-point correlation function, i.e. no extrapolation of the
Pauli form factor
F_{2}(k^{2})
for
k^{2}\to 0
is needed as tried in
earlier attempts in Ref.
Blum:2014oka
.

The HLbL contribution to the muon
(g-2)
from the light quarks can be
obtained from the matrix element of the electromagnetic current

j_{\rho}(x)=\frac{2}{3}(\bar{u}\gamma_{\rho}u)(x)-\frac{1}{3}(\bar{d}\gamma_{\rho}d)(x)-\frac{1}{3}(\bar{s}\gamma_{\rho}s)(x),

(1)

between muon states, which can be parametrized by two form factors
(assuming Lorentz symmetry, current conservation as well as parity
and charge conjugation invariance)

(ie)\langle\mu^{-}(p^{\prime})|j_{\rho}(0)|\mu^{-}(p)\rangle=-(ie)\bar{u}(p^{\prime})\Big[\gamma_{\rho}F_{1}(k^{2})+\frac{\sigma_{\rho\sigma}k_{\sigma}}{2m}F_{2}(k^{2})\Big]u(p),

(2)

where
e
is the electric charge of the electron,
m
is the muon
mass,
\sigma_{\rho\sigma}\equiv\frac{i}{2}[\gamma_{\rho},\gamma_{\sigma}]
and we use
\gamma
-matrices in Euclidean
space with
\{\gamma_{\mu},\gamma_{\nu}\}=2\delta_{\mu\nu}

that are Hermitian,
\gamma_{\mu}^{\dagger}=\gamma_{\mu}
. The
on-shell momenta in Euclidean space fulfill
p^{2}=p^{\prime 2}=-m^{2}
and
the momentum transfer from the external photon is denoted by
k_{\mu}=p_{\mu}^{\prime}-p_{\mu}
. The anomalous magnetic moment is then given by the
Pauli form factor at vanishing momentum transfer
a_{\mu}=F_{2}(0)
.

From the expression for the HLbL diagram in Fig.
1
in
Minkowski space given in Ref.
KN_02
, we obtain the
corresponding result in Euclidean space by performing a Wick rotation
(
\int_{q}\equiv\int\frac{d^{4}q}{(2\pi)^{4}}
)

\displaystyle(ie)\langle\mu^{-}(p^{\prime})|j_{\rho}(0)|\mu^{-}(p)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-ie)^{3}\,(ie)^{4}\int_{q_{1},q_{2}}\frac{1}{q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)^{2}}

(3)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\;\frac{-1}{(p^{\prime}-q_{1})^{2}+m^{2}}\,\frac{-1}{(p^{\prime}-q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}+m^{2}}\phantom{\frac{1}{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\;\bar{u}(p^{\prime})\gamma_{\mu}(i\not{p}^{\prime}-i\not{q}_{1}-m)\gamma_{\nu}(i\not{p}^{\prime}-i\not{q}_{1}-i\not{q}_{2}-m)\gamma_{\lambda}u(p)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\;\Pi_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}(q_{1},q_{2},k-q_{1}-q_{2}),

with the QCD four-point correlation function (
\int_{x}\equiv\int d^{4}x
)

\Pi_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})=\int_{x,y,z}\;e^{-i(q_{1}\cdot x+q_{2}\cdot y+q_{3}\cdot z)}\Big\langle j_{\mu}(x)j_{\nu}(y)j_{\lambda}(z)j_{\rho}(0)\Big\rangle_{\rm QCD}.

(4)

The issue of the Wick rotation in general requires some care.
Starting from the time-ordered correlation function with interpolating
operators for the muon initial and final states,
the standard recipe requires one to Wick rotate the loop momenta, here

q_{1}
and
q_{2}
in Eq. (
3
), and the
external momenta, here
p
and
p^{\prime}
; see the derivation for a general Feynman
diagram in Ref.
Sterman_QFT
. The expression for the loop
integral is initially valid for real Euclidean vectors
p,p^{\prime}

and one needs, in principle, to perform an analytic continuation of
the final result after all loop integrations have been performed from

p^{\prime}{}^{2},p^{2}>0
to
p^{2}=p^{\prime}{}^{2}=-m^{2}
to recover the result for an on-shell
momentum. In Eq. (
3
), we have declared the Euclidean
norm of
p
and
p^{\prime}
to be
-m^{2}
from the outset, and return to the issue around
Eq. (
21
) below.

Since the electromagnetic current is conserved, the tensor

\Pi_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})
satisfies the Ward identities
(momentum conservation entails
q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3}+q_{4}=0
)

\left\{q_{1\mu};q_{2\nu};q_{3\lambda};(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})_{\rho}\right\}\Pi_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})=0.

(5)

This implies the relation
Aldins:1970id

\Pi_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}(q_{1},q_{2},k-q_{1}-q_{2})=-k_{\sigma}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{\rho}}\Pi_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(q_{1},q_{2},k-q_{1}-q_{2}),

(6)

which allows one to pull out the factor
k_{\sigma}
from the vertex
function in Eq. (
3
) to obtain the needed
form factor
F_{2}(0)
with the projection operator
Aldins:1970id

\left.a_{\mu}^{{\rm HLbL}}=F_{2}(0)=\frac{-i}{48m}\trace\{[\gamma_{\rho},\gamma_{\sigma}](-i\not{p}+m)\Gamma_{\rho\sigma}(p,p)(-i\not{p}+m)\}\right|_{p^{2}=-m^{2}}\,.

(7)

The HLbL contribution to the vertex function reads for non-vanishing
momentum transfer

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\rho\sigma}(p^{\prime},p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-e^{6}\int_{q_{1},q_{2}}\frac{1}{q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)^{2}}\frac{1}{(p^{\prime}-q_{1})^{2}+m^{2}}\frac{1}{(p^{\prime}-q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}+m^{2}}

(8)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big(\gamma_{\mu}(i\not{p}^{\prime}-i\not{q}_{1}-m)\gamma_{\nu}(i\not{p}^{\prime}-i\not{q}_{1}-i\not{q}_{2}-m)\gamma_{\lambda}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{\rho}}\Pi_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(q_{1},q_{2},k-q_{1}-q_{2}).

In order go over to a position-space representation for
a_{\mu}^{{\rm HLbL}}
, we insert into Eq. (
8
) the
expression for the four-point function from
Eq. (
4
) and interchange the integrals over
momenta and positions. One can then write the momenta
\not{q}_{1}

and
\not{q}_{2}
in the numerator as derivatives with respect to

x
and
y
of the exponential function in
Eq. (
4
) and also perform the derivative with
respect to
k_{\rho}
to obtain a factor
z_{\rho}
.

In this way one gets the following expression for the vertex function
at vanishing momentum transfer that enters in the projector in
Eq. (
7
) in terms of position-space functions

\Gamma_{\rho\sigma}(p,p)\;=\;-e^{6}\int_{x,y}K_{\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)\,\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y),

(9)

with the QED
kernel
1
1

1

Now that the momentum transfer has been set to zero,
we use the letter
k
to denote an integration variable in
Eq. (
11
).

\displaystyle K_{\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\gamma_{\mu}(i\not{p}+\not{\partial}^{(x)}-m)\gamma_{\nu}(i\not{p}+\not{\partial}^{(x)}+\not{\partial}^{(y)}-m)\gamma_{\lambda}\,\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}\,,

(10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\int_{q,k}\frac{1}{q^{2}\,k^{2}\,(q+k)^{2}}\,\frac{1}{(p-q)^{2}+m^{2}}\,\frac{1}{(p-q-k)^{2}+m^{2}}\;e^{-i(q\cdot x+k\cdot y)}\,.

(11)

The kernel
K_{\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)
in
Eqs. (
9
) and (
10
) is
understood to be a function, not a differential operator.

The function

\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)\;=\;\int_{z}iz_{\rho}\;\Big\langle{j_{\mu}(x)j_{\nu}(y)j_{\sigma}(z)j_{\lambda}(0)}\Big\rangle_{\rm QCD}

(12)

in Eq. (
9
) is a spatial moment of the
four-point function in QCD. Note the order of the indices

\lambda\sigma
on left-hand side and the order of the currents

j_{\sigma}(z)j_{\lambda}(0)
on the right-hand side. We have frequently
used the translation invariance of the four-point function to shift
the integration variables
x,y
or to reverse their direction
z\to-z
. The most important properties of
\widehat{\Pi}

(Bose and reflection symmetries, transversality from current conservation)
are reviewed in subsection
VII.1
.

Note that the function
\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
in
Eq. (
11
) has a logarithmic infrared divergence for
on-shell muon momentum
p^{2}=-m^{2}
inside the loop integration,
i.e. for small
q,k
with the three massless photon propagators and
the two on-shell massive muon propagators. The IR divergence
disappears in the kernel
K_{\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)
, after the
projection on
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
in Eq. (
7
), as
it should be, since the latter is well defined. After the projection,
only terms with derivatives with respect to
x
and / or
y
remain,
which bring down additional factors of
k
and / or
q
from the
exponential in Eq. (
11
). However, since it is
convenient to first compute the scalar function
{\cal I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
, we will regulate the infrared divergence, see details
below. After projecting on
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
the regulator can be
removed.

We insert Eq. (
9
) into
Eq. (
7
), evaluate the trace of the Dirac
matrices and obtain the expression

a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}=\frac{me^{6}}{3}\int_{x,y}{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)\;i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y),

(13)

where the QED kernel is given by

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)

(14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{16m^{2}}{\rm Tr}\Big\{(-i\not{p}+m)[\gamma_{\rho},\gamma_{\sigma}](-i\not{p}+m)K_{\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{8m}{\rm Tr}\Big\{\Big(\not{p}[\gamma_{\rho},\gamma_{\sigma}]+2(p_{\sigma}\gamma_{\rho}-p_{\rho}\gamma_{\sigma})\Big)\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\lambda}\Big\}\,\partial^{(x)}_{\alpha}(\partial^{(x)}_{\beta}+\partial^{(y)}_{\beta})\,{\cal I}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4m}{\rm Tr}\Big\{\Big(\not{p}[\gamma_{\rho},\gamma_{\sigma}]+2(p_{\sigma}\gamma_{\rho}-p_{\rho}\gamma_{\sigma})\Big)\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\nu}\Big\}\;p_{\lambda}\;\partial^{(x)}_{\alpha}\,{\cal I}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4m}{\rm Tr}\Big\{\Big(\not{p}[\gamma_{\rho},\gamma_{\sigma}]+2(p_{\sigma}\gamma_{\rho}-p_{\rho}\gamma_{\sigma})\Big)\gamma_{\mu}(p_{\lambda}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\beta}-p_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\lambda}+p_{\nu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\lambda})\Big\}\;(\partial^{(x)}_{\beta}+\partial^{(y)}_{\beta})\,{\cal I},

and where we used
\not{p}\not{p}=p^{2}=-m^{2}
and

(-i\not{p}+m)(i\not{p}+m)=0
. The use of an IR
regulator in the function
\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
is always
understood. As noted above, only terms with derivatives acting on

\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
survive after projecting on
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
.

It may be worth pointing out some discrete symmetries of the quantities introduced above.
First, we note that, for a general vector
p
,

{\cal I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}={\cal I}(-p,-x,-y)_{\rm IR~reg.}.

(16)

Second, it is easy to show that

\displaystyle{\cal I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\cal I}(-p^{*},x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.})^{*},

(17)

\displaystyle{\cal I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal I}(p,x,x-y)_{\rm IR~reg.},

(18)

whence it follows that

K_{\lambda\nu\mu}(p,x,x-y)=K_{\mu\nu\lambda}(-p^{*},x,y)^{\dagger}.

(19)

Finally, the latter equation entails the following property for the full kernel,

{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\lambda\nu\mu}(p,x,x-y)=-{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(-p^{*},x,y)^{*}.

(20)

Our goal is to perform as many integrations of the 8-dimensional
integral over
x,y
in Eq. (
13
) as possible
(semi-) analytically to have full control over the QED kernel function

{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)
. To achieve this, we
will rewrite the function
\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
in
Eq. (
11
) in terms of position-space
propagators
Position_space_methods_old
;
Position_space_methods_new
and use the method of Gegenbauer
polynomials
Gegenbauer_momentum_space_early
;
Gegenbauer_momentum_space
;
Gegenbauer_momentum_space_dim_reg
;
Roskies_et_al_90
;
Gegenbauer_position_space
to perform the angular
integrals and average over the direction of the muon
momentum
Jegerlehner:2009ry
(see also Ref.
Roskies_et_al_90
;
Barbieri:1974nc
). We will show the details
of this calculation in the next sections, but present here first the
structure of the final result, our master formula for
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
in position space.

As mentioned earlier, we adopt an approach where the Euclidean vector
p
obeys
p^{2}=-m^{2}

from the outset, exploiting the fact that the muon is the ground state in the channel of its symmetry.
In the context of the use of Gegenbauer polynomials for loop integrals in
momentum space in Ref.
Gegenbauer_momentum_space
this procedure
only works in a straightforward way, if the integrand is a meromorphic
function of all the integration variables and all the external
invariants, like
p^{2}
. Fortunately, in our case one can show that the relevant
p
-dependent part of the
integrand is a meromorphic function and the analytical
continuation to
p^{2}=-m^{2}
can be performed without
problems.
Aiming at keeping the absolute size of the imaginary components as small as possible,
we thus parametrize the on-shell momentum as follows,

p=im\hat{\epsilon},\quad\hat{\epsilon}^{2}=1,\quad p^{2}=-m^{2},

(21)

where the unit vector
\hat{\epsilon}
parametrizes the direction of the muon
momentum.
From Eq. (
17
),
for a vector
p
with purely imaginary components,
{\cal I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
is real,
and so is
2
2

2

Indeed, the trace of a product of
linear combinations of Euclidean Dirac matrices with real coefficients is real.

{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)
.
Furthermore, the general property Eq. (
20
) becomes

{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\lambda\nu\mu}(p,x,x-y)=-{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y),\qquad p=im\hat{\epsilon}.

(22)

Since
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
is a Lorentz scalar, the expression in
Eq. (
13
) can be averaged over the direction

\hat{\epsilon}
of the muon momentum (
\int d\Omega_{\hat{\epsilon}}=2\pi^{2}
),

\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int d\Omega_{\hat{\epsilon}}\,{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)\equiv\Big\langle{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)\Big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}.

(23)

In this way we obtain

\displaystyle\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal G}^{\rm I}_{\delta[\rho,\sigma]\mu\alpha\nu\beta\lambda}\,\langle\hat{\epsilon}_{\delta}\partial^{(x)}_{\alpha}(\partial^{(x)}_{\beta}+\partial^{(y)}_{\beta}){\cal I}\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}

(24)

\displaystyle+m\,{\cal G}^{\rm II}_{\delta[\rho,\sigma]\mu\alpha\nu\beta\lambda}\langle\hat{\epsilon}_{\delta}\hat{\epsilon}_{\beta}\;\partial^{(x)}_{\alpha}{\cal I}\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle+m\,{\cal G}^{\rm III}_{\delta[\rho,\sigma]\mu\alpha\nu\beta\lambda}\;\langle\hat{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\hat{\epsilon}_{\delta}(\partial^{(x)}_{\beta}+\partial^{(y)}_{\beta}){\cal I}\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}},

where we have defined

\displaystyle{\cal G}^{\rm I}_{\delta[\rho,\sigma]\mu\alpha\nu\beta\lambda}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{8}}{\rm Tr}\Big\{\Big(\gamma_{\delta}[\gamma_{\rho},\gamma_{\sigma}]+2(\delta_{\delta\sigma}\gamma_{\rho}-\delta_{\delta\rho}\gamma_{\sigma})\Big)\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\lambda}\Big\},

(25)

\displaystyle{\cal G}^{\rm II}_{\delta[\rho,\sigma]\mu\alpha\nu\beta\lambda}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}{\rm Tr}\Big\{\Big(\gamma_{\delta}[\gamma_{\rho},\gamma_{\sigma}]+2(\delta_{\delta\sigma}\gamma_{\rho}-\delta_{\delta\rho}\gamma_{\sigma})\Big)\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\nu}\Big\}\;\delta_{\beta\lambda},

(26)

\displaystyle{\cal G}^{\rm III}_{\delta[\rho,\sigma]\mu\alpha\nu\beta\lambda}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}{\rm Tr}\Big\{\Big(\gamma_{\delta}[\gamma_{\rho},\gamma_{\sigma}]+2(\delta_{\delta\sigma}\gamma_{\rho}-\delta_{\delta\rho}\gamma_{\sigma})\Big)\gamma_{\mu}(\delta_{\alpha\lambda}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\beta}-\delta_{\alpha\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\lambda}+\delta_{\alpha\nu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\lambda})\Big\}.\qquad

(27)

The tensors
{\cal G}^{\rm A}_{\delta[\rho,\sigma]\mu\alpha\nu\beta\lambda}
are sums of
products of Kronecker deltas from the traces of the Dirac matrices in
Euclidean space.
The QED kernel
\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)
inherits
from the kernel
{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(p,x,y)
the antisymmetry property

\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\lambda\nu\mu}(x,x-y)=-\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)

(28)

under the transformation (
\mu\leftrightarrow\lambda
,
y\to x-y
)
upon averaging both sides of Eq. (
22
) over the direction of the muon momentum.

We thus arrive at our master formula for
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
in
position space (which we have already presented previously in
Refs.
Talk_Asmussen_DPG_2015
;
Green:2015mva
;
Asmussen:2016lse
)

\displaystyle a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{me^{6}}{3}\int_{x,y}\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)\;i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y),

(29)

\displaystyle\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\rm A}={\rm I,II,III}}{\cal G}^{\rm A}_{\delta[\rho,\sigma]\mu\alpha\nu\beta\lambda}T^{\rm A}_{\alpha\beta\delta}(x,y),

(30)

\displaystyle i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{z}z_{\rho}\,\Big\langle\,j_{\mu}(x)\,j_{\nu}(y)\,j_{\sigma}(z)\,j_{\lambda}(0)\Big\rangle_{\rm QCD}.

(31)

After contracting the Lorentz indices in
Eq. (
29
), the integration reduces to a
3-dimensional integral over
|x|,|y|
and
x\cdot y
.
For illustration we depict in
Fig.
2
the HLbL diagram in the muon
(g-2)

indicating the positions
x,y,z,0
of the four vector currents
(attached to the photons) in the master formula in
Eq. (
29
).
We emphasize at this point that the kernel
\bar{\cal L}
is far from being
unique. A significant amount of freedom remains to adjust the kernel
to the needs of practical calculations, wihtout modifying the final
value of
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
. We return to this aspect in subsection

VIII.1
.

Figure 2:
Hadronic light-by-light scattering diagram in the muon
(g-2)

with the locations of the vertices
x,y,z,0
in the master formula
in Eq. (
29
).

The tensors
T^{\rm A}_{\alpha\beta\delta}(x,y)
in
Eq. (
30
) can be decomposed into a scalar
S
, a
vector
V_{\delta}
and a tensor part
T_{\beta\delta}
:

\displaystyle T^{\rm I}_{\alpha\beta\delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial^{(x)}_{\alpha}(\partial^{(x)}_{\beta}+\partial^{(y)}_{\beta})V_{\delta}(x,y),

(32)

\displaystyle T^{\rm II}_{\alpha\beta\delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m\partial^{(x)}_{\alpha}\Big(T_{\beta\delta}(x,y)+\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\beta\delta}S(x,y)\Big),

(33)

\displaystyle T^{\rm III}_{\alpha\beta\delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m(\partial^{(x)}_{\beta}+\partial^{(y)}_{\beta})\Big(T_{\alpha\delta}(x,y)+\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\alpha\delta}S(x,y)\Big).

(34)

These parts are given in terms of the function

\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
from Eq. (
11
) as follows
(see Eq. (
24
)):

\displaystyle S(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm{IR~reg.}}\Big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}},

(35)

\displaystyle V_{\delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle\hat{\epsilon}_{\delta}\;\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm{IR~reg.}}\Big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}},

(36)

\displaystyle T_{\beta\delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Big(\hat{\epsilon}_{\beta}\hat{\epsilon}_{\delta}-\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\beta\delta}\Big)\;\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm{IR~reg.}}\Big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}.

(37)

From the property (
16
) of the scalar function
{\cal I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
,
it follows that
S(x,y)
and
T_{\beta\delta}(x,y)
are even under the simultaneous sign
reflection of both their arguments, while
V_{\delta}(x,y)
is odd. From here, it follows that
the
T_{\alpha\beta\delta}^{\rm A}(x,y)
are all odd under
(x,y)\to(-x,-y)
, so that the QED kernel
is too,

\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)=-\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(-x,-y).

(38)

Since the function
{\cal I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
is ultraviolet finite by power-counting (including at
x=y=0
),
we conclude that so is the QED kernel, and Eq. (
38
) then implies the property

\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(0,0)=0.

(39)

The quantity
S(x,y)
can only be a scalar function
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}

of the three invariants
|x|,x\cdot y,|y|
; we call this a weight function. The vector and
tensor functions will be parametrized by respectively two and three weight functions:

\displaystyle S(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}(|x|,x\cdot y,|y|),\phantom{\frac{1}{1}}

(40)

\displaystyle V_{\delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{\delta}\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}(|x|,x\cdot y,|y|)+y_{\delta}\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(2)}(|x|,x\cdot y,|y|),

(41)

\displaystyle T_{\alpha\beta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x_{\alpha}x_{\beta}-\frac{x^{2}}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\;\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(1)}(|x|,x\cdot y,|y|)+(y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}-\frac{y^{2}}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\;\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}(|x|,x\cdot y,|y|)

(42)

\displaystyle+(x_{\alpha}y_{\beta}+y_{\alpha}x_{\beta}-\frac{x\cdot y}{2}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\;\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(3)}(|x|,x\cdot y,|y|).

In total, the QED kernel
\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)
in Eq. (
30
) is
thus parametrized by six weight functions and their derivatives.
See Appendix
A
for the explicit expressions of the tensors
T^{\rm A}_{\alpha\beta\delta}(x,y)
.
As we will see in the explicit calculation later, the IR divergence of

\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
will only be important in the scalar
weight function
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}(|x|,x\cdot y,|y|)
,
before performing the derivatives in Eqs. (
33
) and
(
34
).

It is clear that the tensors
S(x,y)
,
V_{\delta}(x,y)
and
T_{\beta\delta}(x,y)

inherit from
{\cal I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
the invariance under
y\to(x-y)
.
In turn, their invariance implies the following symmetry properties for the weight functions,

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)},

(43)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}+\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(2)},

(44)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(2)}_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(2)},

(45)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(1)}_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(1)}+\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}+2\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(3)},

(46)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)},

(47)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(3)}_{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}-\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(3)}.

(48)

Unstarred functions have as argument
(|x|,x\cdot y,|y|)
, while starred functions refer to the same weight functions but
with argument
(|x|,x\cdot(x-y),|x-y|)
.
Furthermore, the rank-three tensors contributing to the QED kernel
satisfy
3
3

3

In fact, the contributions
S(x,y)
and
T_{\beta\delta}(x,y)

to the rank-three tensors separately satisfy Eq. (
50
).

\displaystyle T^{\rm I}_{\beta\alpha\delta}(x,x-y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{\rm I}_{\alpha\beta\delta}(x,y),

(49)

\displaystyle T^{\rm III}_{\beta\alpha\delta}(x,x-y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{\rm II}_{\alpha\beta\delta}(x,y).

(50)

We already know that the QED kernel as a whole obeys the antisymmetry property Eq. (
28
)
under the transformation (
\mu\leftrightarrow\lambda
,
y\to x-y
).
Eq. (
49
), combined with the fact that

{\cal G}^{\rm I}
is antisymmetric under the simultaneous index exchanges

\mu\leftrightarrow\lambda
and
\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta
, implies that
the contribution to the QED kernel
{\cal G}^{\rm I}T^{\rm I}

by itself is antisymmetric under the transformation (
\mu\leftrightarrow\lambda
,
y\to x-y
).

III
Preparatory steps for the calculation of the QED weight functions
in position space

In this section, we present some (partly known) results on propagators
and their expansion in Gegenbauer polynomials. These preliminaries
will allow us to provide the expansion in Gegenbauer polynomials of
the function
J(\hat{\epsilon},u)
, defined in Eq. (
55
),
which plays a crucial role in the entire calculation.
Then, starting in subsection
III.4
,
we perform the average over the direction of the muon momentum analytically.
The final convolution integrals yielding the tensors

S
,
V_{\delta}
and
T_{\beta\delta}
are treated in sections

IV
and
V
.

III.1
Starting point for the calculation of

\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}

To obtain a convenient expression for the scalar function

\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
in Eq. (
11
), we
translate the momentum integrals into position-space perturbation
theory integrals, where the integration variables correspond to the
positions of the vertices, see
Fig.
3
.

Figure 3:
Feynman diagram of the QED part of the HLbL contribution in
the muon
g-2
in position space, see
Fig.
2
, corresponding to the scalar function

\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
in Eq. (
11
). Note that
the arrows on the muon line are only meant for illustration; all
propagators are scalar functions.
In the derivation, the Euclidean momentum
p
flowing through the diagram was assumed to be real.

The relevant Fourier transforms can be performed by using the
well-known expressions for the massless and massive propagators in
position space
Bogoliubov_Shirkov
:
4
4

4

The positions
x,y

in the definitions of the propagators are generic Euclidean
four-vectors and do not correspond to the vertices in the Feynman
diagrams in Figs.
2
and

3
.

\displaystyle G_{0}(x-y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{k}\frac{e^{ik\cdot(x-y)}}{k^{2}}=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}(x-y)^{2}}\,,

(51)

\displaystyle G_{m}(x-y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{k}\frac{e^{ik\cdot(x-y)}}{k^{2}+m^{2}}=\frac{m}{4\pi^{2}|x-y|}K_{1}(m|x-y|)\,,

(52)

where
K_{1}
is a modified Bessel function and we use the conventions
from Ref.
Abramowitz_Stegun
. The propagators in position space
are Green’s functions of the ‘Euclidean Klein-Gordon’ equation,

(-\triangle^{(x)}+m^{2})G_{m}(x-y)=\delta^{(4)}(x-y),

(53)

and analogously for
m=0
. Here
\triangle^{(x)}=\sum_{\mu=0}^{3}\partial_{\mu}^{(x)}\partial_{\mu}^{(x)}

is the four-dimensional Laplacian.

These position-space representations of propagators have been used for
a long time to evaluate Feynman diagrams, see for instance
Refs.
Position_space_methods_old
;
Gegenbauer_position_space
;
Position_space_methods_new
. However, these calculations were mostly
dealing with loop integrals, not their Fourier transform as in
Eq. (
11
); they involved massless particles (relevant for QCD)
and were either aimed at evaluating renormalization constants at higher loop order, or
treated Feynman diagrams with a special topology, e.g. of the
sun-rise type, where
L+1
propagators connect the two points
x
and

y
in a
L
-loop diagram. Thus our goal required the development of
additional computational methods.

We then obtain as the starting point for the evaluation of the
weight-functions of the QED kernel in position space the following
representation of the scalar function
\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
for

p=im\hat{\epsilon}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{u}\,G_{0}(y-u)\,J(\hat{\epsilon},u)\,J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\,,

(54)

\displaystyle J(\hat{\epsilon},u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{u}}\,G_{0}(u-\tilde{u})e^{m\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\tilde{u}}\,G_{m}(\tilde{u})\,.

(55)

Recall again the need to regulate the IR divergence of the function

\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
, which is related in position space to the
behavior of the integrand in the final integration in
Eq. (
54
) for large
|u|
, i.e. at long
distances. Below we will show that
J(\hat{\epsilon},u)\sim 1/|u|
for
large
|u|
and therefore the integral for
\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}

is logarithmically divergent for large
|u|
, since
\int_{u}\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}d|u||u|^{3}d\Omega_{\hat{u}}
(with unit-vector
\hat{u}=u/|u|
) and
G_{0}(y-u)\sim 1/u^{2}
.

In the derivation of Eq. (
54
) we encounter the
integral

\int_{k}\frac{e^{ik\cdot x}}{(k-p)^{2}+m^{2}}=e^{ix\cdot p}\,G_{m}(x)=e^{-m\hat{\epsilon}\cdot x}\,G_{m}(x)\,.

(56)

The first equality follows, formally, by shifting the integration
variable
k\to k-p
. The derivation is formal because with our
parametrization of the on-shell momentum
p=im\hat{\epsilon}
in the
integrand,
p
is a complex Euclidean vector, while the shift in the
integration variable assumes that
p
is a real Euclidean vector. The
final result, also used in Eq. (
55
), follows by
analytical continuation of the expression from a real off-shell vector

p
to a complex on-shell vector
p
. The second equality with the
on-shell momentum
p
can be derived directly by using the Schwinger
representation of the propagator
((k-p)^{2}+m^{2})^{-1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}d\alpha\,\exp(-\alpha((k-p)^{2}+m^{2}))
and then, after the
dependence on the components of
k_{\mu}
has been factorized,
performing four simple Gaussian integrals.

Note that although the Feynman diagram from
Fig.
3
is only a tree-level diagram
and thus trivial in momentum space, the expression in position space
in Eq. (
54
) is a non-trivial convolution of
propagators and exponentials.

III.2
Gegenbauer method for angular integrals in position space
in four dimensions

We summarize first some basic properties of Gegenbauer polynomials
which have been used since a long time ago to perform the angular
integrations of Feynman loop integrals (hyperspherical approach) in
momentum space
Gegenbauer_momentum_space
and in position
space
Gegenbauer_position_space
. Since we work in
d=4
dimensions,
we only need the special case of the Gegenbauer polynomials
C_{n}(\xi)\equiv C_{n}^{(\alpha=1)}(\xi)\equiv U_{n}(\xi)
, which are actually
equal to the Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind, see
Ref.
Abramowitz_Stegun
.
5
5

5

Note that in some references

C_{n}(\xi)
denotes a dilated Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind.

The corresponding generating function is given by

\frac{1}{\tau^{2}-2\xi\tau+1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\tau^{n}\,C_{n}(\xi),\quad-1\leq\xi\leq 1,\ \ |\tau|<1.

(57)

Some low-order Gegenbauer polynomials are given by
C_{0}(\xi)=1
,

C_{1}(\xi)=2\xi
,
C_{2}(\xi)=4\xi^{2}-1
and therefore
\xi=C_{1}(\xi)/2
,
\xi^{2}=\left(C_{2}(\xi)+C_{0}(\xi)\right)/4
.

Some simple properties of the Gegenbauer polynomials (Chebyshev
polynomials of the second kind) are
Abramowitz_Stegun

\displaystyle C_{n}(\cos\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sin((n+1)\theta)}{\sin(\theta)},

(58)

\displaystyle C_{n}(-\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{n}C_{n}(\xi),

(59)

\displaystyle C_{n}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n+1,

(60)

\displaystyle\xi\,C_{n}(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big[C_{n+1}(\xi)+(1-\delta_{n0})C_{n-1}(\xi)\Big],\qquad n\geq 0,

(61)

\displaystyle\xi^{2}C_{n}(\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\Big[C_{n+2}(\xi)+(2-\delta_{n0})C_{n}(\xi)+C_{n-2}(\xi)\Big],\qquad n\geq 0,

(62)

where we adopt in Eqs. (
61
) and (
62
) the
convention that
C_{n}(\xi)\equiv 0
for
n\leq-1
. The property
(
59
) under parity transformations and the
normalization (
60
) follow easily from the
generating function in Eq. (
57
).

For the evaluation of angular integrals in Feynman diagrams one makes
use of the fact that the Gegenbauer polynomials (hyperspherical
polynomials) are the polynomials that obey the orthogonality relations

\displaystyle\Big\langle C_{n}(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{x})\;C_{m}(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{y})\Big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\delta_{nm}}{n+1}\,C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y}),

\displaystyle\Big\langle C_{n}(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{x})\;C_{m}(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{x})\Big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\delta_{nm}},

(63)

on the unit sphere,
where we denote unit four-vectors as
\hat{x}=x/|x|,\hat{y}=y/|y|
.

III.3
Expansion in Gegenbauer polynomials of propagators in
position space, of
the exponential function and of the function
J(\hat{\epsilon},y)

For our goal to perform the angular integrations in the function

\mathcal{I}(p,x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}
in Eq. (
54
) and the
averages in Eqs (
35
), (
36
)
and (
37
), it is important to note that if we have
a function
f=f(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot x,x^{2})
, its dependence on the
angle between the vectors
\hat{\epsilon}
and
x
can be expanded in
Gegenbauer polynomials as follows

\displaystyle f(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot x,x^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\zeta_{n}(x^{2})\,C_{n}(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{x}),

(64)

\displaystyle\zeta_{n}(x^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle f(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot x,x^{2})\,C_{n}(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{x})\Big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}},

(65)

where one uses the orthogonality relations in
Eq. (
63
) to derive the expression for

\zeta_{n}(x^{2})
.

For later reference we write down the expansions into Gegenbauer
polynomials for the massless and massive propagators in position
space, the exponential function and the function
J(p,y)
from
Eq. (
55
) (again, the vectors
x,y
in the propagators
and in the exponential function are meant to be generic Euclidean
four-vectors)

\displaystyle 4\pi^{2}G_{0}(x-y)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(x-y)^{2}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_{n}(x^{2},y^{2})\,C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y}),

(66)

\displaystyle d_{n}(x^{2},y^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta(x^{2}-y^{2})\frac{|y|^{n}}{|x|^{n+2}}+\theta(y^{2}-x^{2})\frac{|x|^{n}}{|y|^{n+2}},

(67)

\displaystyle G_{m}(x-y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\gamma_{n}(x^{2},y^{2})\,C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y}),

(68)

\displaystyle\gamma_{n}(x^{2},y^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n+1}{2\pi^{2}|x||y|}\Big(\theta(x^{2}-y^{2})\,I_{n+1}(m|y|)\,K_{n+1}(m|x|)

(69)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\ \ +\theta(y^{2}-x^{2})\,I_{n+1}(m|x|)\,K_{n+1}(m|y|)\Big),

\displaystyle e^{\hat{\epsilon}\cdot x}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle e^{x\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{x}}=2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)\frac{I_{n+1}(|x|)}{|x|}\,C_{n}(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{x}),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{4},

(70)

\displaystyle J(\hat{\epsilon},u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}(u^{2})\,C_{n}(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{u}),

(71)

\displaystyle z_{n}(u^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\Big[I_{n+2}(m|u|)\frac{K_{0}(m|u|)}{n+1}+I_{n+1}(m|u|)\Big(\frac{K_{1}(m|u|)}{n+1}+\frac{K_{0}(m|u|)}{m|u|}\Big)\Big],

(72)

where
I_{n}
is another modified Bessel function, see
Ref.
Abramowitz_Stegun
. For integrals involving modified Bessel functions,
we have also found the further references
DLMF Website
;
Gradshteyn_Ryzhik
;
Watson
;
Prudnikov
useful.

The expansion of the massless propagator
G_{0}(x-y)
in
position space in Eqs. (
66
) and (
67
)
is formally equal to the corresponding expansion in momentum space and
follows immediately from the use of the generating function of the
Gegenbauer polynomials in Eq. (
57
) to
write down the expansion of the massive propagator in Euclidean
momentum space and then performing the limit
m\to 0

KN_02
.

The expansion into Gegenbauer polynomials for the massive propagator

G_{m}(x-y)
can be derived as follows. It satisfies the differential
equation from Eq. (
53
). Since
G_{m}(x-y)
is a
scalar function, we can choose the coordinate system such that
y

lies along the positive
\hat{e}_{0}
axis, so that
x
has coordinates

(|x|\cos\phi_{1},|x|\sin\phi_{1},0,0)
and
\cos\phi_{1}
coincides with
\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y}
.
The four-dimensional Laplacian operator for a function of
|x|
and

\cos\phi_{1}
reads

\triangle^{(x)}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial|x|^{2}}+\frac{3}{|x|}\frac{\partial}{\partial|x|}+\frac{1}{|x|^{2}\sin^{2}\phi_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi_{1}}\Big(\sin^{2}\phi_{1}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi_{1}}\Big).

(73)

The important fact is that the Gegenbauer polynomials (Chebyshev
polynomials of the second kind) are eigenfunctions of the angular part
of the Laplacian operator

\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\phi_{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi_{1}}\Big(\sin^{2}\phi_{1}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi_{1}}C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1})\Big)=-n(n+2)C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1}).

(74)

Thus inserting the expansion of
G_{m}(x-y)
in
Eq. (
68
) into the Klein-Gordon equation in
Eq. (
53
) and applying the differential operator
term by term yields the condition

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big\{-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial|x|^{2}}-\frac{3}{|x|}\frac{\partial}{\partial|x|}+m^{2}+\frac{n(n+2)}{|x|^{2}}\Big\}\gamma_{n}(x^{2},y^{2})\,C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y})=\delta^{(4)}(x-y).

(75)

Using the general expression

\delta^{(4)}(x-y)=\frac{1}{|x|^{3}}\delta(|x|-|y|)\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\phi_{1}}\delta(\phi_{1}-\phi_{1}^{y})\frac{1}{\sin\phi_{2}}\delta(\phi_{2}-\phi_{2}^{y})\delta(\phi_{3}-\phi_{3}^{y}),

(76)

we integrate both sides of Eq. (
75
) over
\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{2}\sin\phi_{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi_{3}
and use the completeness
relation of the Gegenbauer polynomials,

\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1})C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1}^{y})=\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\phi_{1}}\delta(\phi_{1}-\phi_{1}^{y})

(77)

to obtain

\displaystyle 4\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big\{-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial|x|^{2}}-\frac{3}{|x|}\frac{\partial}{\partial|x|}+m^{2}+\frac{n(n+2)}{|x|^{2}}\Big\}\gamma_{n}(x^{2},y^{2})\,C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|x|^{3}}\delta(|x|-|y|)\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1}^{y})\,C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1}).

(78)

Choosing again
y
to lie along the
\hat{e}_{0}
direction, we have
\phi_{1}^{y}=0
,

\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y}=\cos\phi_{1}
and
C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1}^{y})=(n+1)
;
comparing the series term by term we obtain the differential equation

\Big\{-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial|x|^{2}}-\frac{3}{|x|}\frac{\partial}{\partial|x|}+m^{2}+\frac{n(n+2)}{|x|^{2}}\Big\}\gamma_{n}(x^{2},y^{2})=\frac{n+1}{2\pi^{2}|x|^{3}}\delta(|x|-|y|).

(79)

Two solutions of the homogeneous equation are
K_{n+1}(m|x|)/|x|
and

I_{n+1}(m|x|)/|x|
. This then leads to the expression for
\gamma_{n}(x^{2},y^{2})
given
in Eq. (
69
).

We are not aware of any paper, where the expansion into Gegenbauer
polynomials of the massive propagator in position space in
Eqs. (
68
) and (
69
) has been
given, even though it is a special case of Gegenbauer’s Addition Theorem
(see p. 365 of Ref.
Watson
).
Note that at
|x|=|y|
, the function
\gamma_{n}(x^{2},y^{2})
is
continuous, but not differentiable - there is a cusp. For
m\to 0
we
recover from the expansion of the massive propagator the expansion for
the massless propagator.

The expansion into Gegenbauer polynomials for the exponential function
in Eq. (
70
) follows from the generating function
of the Bessel functions
I_{n}
and the associated series given in
Ref.
Abramowitz_Stegun

e^{z\cos\theta}=I_{0}(z)+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}I_{n}(z)\cos(n\theta),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C},

(80)

by taking the derivative with respect to
\theta
of both sides of
Eq. (
80
) and then using
Eq. (
58
),
z=|x|
and
\cos\theta=\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{x}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Similar
expressions have already been given in
Refs.
Position_space_methods_old
;
Gegenbauer_momentum_space_dim_reg
;
Gegenbauer_position_space
.

For the derivation of the expansion into Gegenbauer polynomials of

J(\hat{\epsilon},u)
in Eqs. (
71
) and (
72
)
we start from the definition of the function in
Eq. (
55
)

\displaystyle J(\hat{\epsilon},u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{v}G_{0}(u-v)\,e^{m\hat{\epsilon}\cdot v}G_{m}(v)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}(u^{2})\,C_{n}(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{u}).

(81)

Inserting the expansions of the massless propagator

G_{0}(u-v)
from Eqs. (
66
) and (
67
) and
of the exponential
e^{m\hat{\epsilon}\cdot v}
from
Eq. (
70
), we can use the orthogonality properties
of the Gegenbauer polynomials from
Eq. (
63
) to project on the coefficients

z_{n}(u^{2})
as in Eq. (
65
) to obtain the intermediate
result

z_{n}(u^{2})=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{(m|u|)^{n+2}}\int_{0}^{m|u|}\!dt\,t^{n+1}I_{n+1}(t)K_{1}(t)+(m|u|)^{n}\int_{m|u|}^{\infty}\!dt\frac{I_{n+1}(t)}{t^{n+1}}K_{1}(t)\right].

(82)

For the first integral, we use integration by parts, starting from the
observation
K_{1}(t)=-K_{0}^{\prime}(t)
, and then using the well-known
identity
\frac{d}{dt}(t^{n+1}I_{n+1}(t))=t^{n+1}I_{n}(t)
. We then
use the following primitives:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Big\{t^{n+2}\Big(K_{0}(t)I_{n}(t)+K_{1}(t)I_{n+1}(t)\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(n+1)t^{n+1}I_{n}(t)K_{0}(t),

(83)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Big\{\frac{1}{t^{n}}\Big(K_{0}(t)I_{n}(t)+K_{1}(t)I_{n+1}(t)\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2(n+1)}{t^{n+1}}\;I_{n+1}(t)K_{1}(t),

(84)

to obtain the result in Eq. (
72
) after some slight
rearrangements.

It is worth recording the asymptotics of the coefficients
z_{n}(u^{2})
for fixed
n
.
Using the known asymptotics of the modified Bessel functions, one finds

\displaystyle z_{n}(u^{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u^{2}\to 0}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\left(m|u|\right)^{n}}{2^{n+3}\pi^{2}\Gamma(n+2)}\left[\log\left(\frac{m|u|}{2}\right)+\gamma_{\rm E}-\frac{1}{n+1}\right]+{\rm O}\left(|u|^{n+2}\right)

(85)

\displaystyle z_{n}(u^{2})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u^{2}\to\infty}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{n+1}{4\pi^{2}m|u|}\Big(\frac{1}{(n+1)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2m|u|}+{\rm O}(u^{-2})\Big).

(86)

Furthermore, it is worth noting that

\frac{d}{d|u|}(|u|z_{n}(u^{2}))=\frac{(n+1)I_{n+1}(m|u|)K_{0}(m|u|)}{4\pi^{2}m|u|},

(87)

and therefore, resumming the expansion in the angular variable using
Eq. (
70
),

\frac{d}{d|u|}(|u|J(\hat{\epsilon},u))=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}e^{m\hat{\epsilon}\cdot u}K_{0}(m|u|).

(88)

Since
J(\hat{\epsilon},u)
is logarithmically divergent for
|u|\to 0
, as can be seen from the definition in Eq. (
55
),
we have
\lim_{|u|\to 0}(|u|J(\hat{\epsilon},u))=0
. Therefore we can
solve this differential equation by simple integration to obtain the
integral representation
6
6

6

A very similar function appears in Ref.
Blum:2017cer
.

J(\hat{\epsilon},u)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}m|u|}\int_{0}^{m|u|}dt\;e^{t\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{u}}\;K_{0}(t).

(89)

From here it is straightforward to obtain the asymptotics of

J(\hat{\epsilon},u)
at small and large
|u|
.

As already mentioned, around the origin
|u|\to 0
, the function

J(\hat{\epsilon},u)
is logarithmically divergent. The precise behavior
reads:

J(\hat{\epsilon},u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|u|\to 0}}{{\sim}}-\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\log(m^{2}u^{2})+\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}(1-\gamma_{\rm E}+\log(2))+{\rm O}(|u|\log|u|).

(90)

We note the exactly computable special case if the vector
u
is
collinear with
\hat{\epsilon}

J(\hat{\epsilon},\pm|u|\hat{\epsilon})=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\Big(e^{\pm m|u|}(K_{0}(m|u|)\pm K_{1}(m|u|))\mp\frac{1}{m|u|}\Big).

(91)

In this case, the behavior for large
|u|
reads

J(\hat{\epsilon},+|u|\hat{\epsilon})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|u|\to\infty}}{{\sim}}\frac{1}{4\pi\sqrt{2\pi m|u|}},\qquad\qquad J(\hat{\epsilon},-|u|\hat{\epsilon})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|u|\to\infty}}{{\sim}}\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}m|u|}.

(92)

For a generic direction of
u
, we obtain for large
|u|
:

J(\hat{\epsilon},u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|u|\to\infty}}{{\sim}}\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}m|u|}\frac{\pi-\theta}{\sin\theta},\qquad\cos\theta=\hat{\epsilon}\cdot\hat{u},\quad 0<\theta<\pi.

(93)

This behavior at large
|u|
is obtained from Eq. (
89
)
by writing the integral
\int_{0}^{m|u|}=\int_{0}^{\infty}-\int_{m|u|}^{\infty}
.
The first term then yields Eq. (
93
). The second,
using the large-argument expansion of the Bessel function,
yields a correction suppressed by
\exp(-2\sin^{2}(\theta/2)m|u|)
.
In particular, the special ‘collinear’ sector around
\theta=0
, in which
the function
J(\hat{\epsilon},u)
only falls off like
(m|u|)^{-1/2}
, has an angular
size
\Delta\theta\sim(m|u|)^{-1/2}
.

III.4
Average over the direction of the muon momentum

The product
J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)
can be viewed as a
function of
\hat{\epsilon}
on the unit three-sphere, and can therefore
be expanded in scalar, vector, rank-two traceless tensor,
etc. components. Computationally, in order to compute
S
,
V_{\delta}

and
T_{\beta\delta}
in
Eqs. (
35
)-(
37
), it is
simpler to first extract these components before performing the
convolution with the massless propagator. We therefore introduce the
notation

\displaystyle s(x,u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\Big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}},

(94)

\displaystyle v_{\delta}(x,u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle\hat{\epsilon}_{\delta}\;J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\Big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}},

(95)

\displaystyle t_{\beta\delta}(x,u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle\Big(\hat{\epsilon}_{\beta}\hat{\epsilon}_{\delta}-\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\beta\delta}\Big)J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\Big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}},

(96)

so that

\displaystyle S(x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{u}G_{0}(u-y)\,s(x,u),

(97)

\displaystyle V_{\delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{u}G_{0}(u-y)\,v_{\delta}(x,u),

(98)

\displaystyle T_{\beta\delta}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{u}G_{0}(u-y)\,t_{\beta\delta}(x,u).

(99)

III.5
Calculation of
s(x,u)

Inserting into Eq. (
94
) the expansions of
J(\hat{\epsilon},u)
and
J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)
in Gegenbauer polynomials from
Eq. (
71
) and using the orthogonality relations in
Eq. (
63
), the angular average yields

s(x,u)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})\,\frac{C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u})}{n+1}.

(100)

We remark that the symmetry
s(x,u)=s(x,x-u)
, obvious in Eq. (
94
),
remains manifest in Eq. (
100
).
At
x=0
, we have

s(0,u)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\;z_{n}(u^{2})^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|u|\to\infty}}{{\sim}}\frac{1}{(4\pi^{2}m)^{2}u^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(n+1)^{2}}=\frac{1}{192\pi^{2}m^{2}u^{2}},

(101)

where we used the first term in the large-argument expansion (
86
) of
z_{n}(u^{2})
.
Eq. (
101
) provides the leading behavior
of
s(x,u)
at large
|u|
that will be important to deal with the IR
divergence in
S(x,y)
.
We remark that the asymptotic prediction of Eq. (
101
) for
s(x,u)

can also be obtained directly from its definition (
94
), employing the asymptotic form
(
93
) of the function
J(\hat{\epsilon},u)
.

III.6
Calculation of
v_{\delta}(x,u)

We parametrize the vector components in Eq. (
95
) as
follows:

v_{\delta}(x,u)=x_{\delta}\;\mathfrak{f}^{(1)}(x^{2},x\cdot u,u^{2})+u_{\delta}\;\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(x^{2},x\cdot u,u^{2}).

(102)

Multiplying with
x_{\delta}
and
u_{\delta}
and solving the system of
two equations, we get

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{f}^{(1)}\\
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{x^{2}u^{2}-(x\cdot u)^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c@{~~}c}u^{2}&-u\cdot x\\
-u\cdot x&x^{2}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\langle(x\cdot\hat{\epsilon})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}\\
\langle(u\cdot\hat{\epsilon})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}\end{array}\right).

Using Eq. (
61
) and the orthogonality
relations (
63
) we obtain the results

\displaystyle\langle(x\cdot\hat{\epsilon})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}=\frac{|u|}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z_{n-1}(u^{2})+z_{n+1}(u^{2}))\,\frac{z_{n}((x-u)^{2})}{n+1}\,C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{|x-u|}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z_{n-1}((x-u)^{2})+z_{n+1}((x-u)^{2}))\,\frac{z_{n}(u^{2})}{n+1}\,C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u}),

(110)

\displaystyle\langle(u\cdot\hat{\epsilon})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}=\frac{|u|}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(z_{n-1}(u^{2})+z_{n+1}(u^{2}))\,\frac{z_{n}((x-u)^{2})}{n+1}\,C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u}),

with the convention that
z_{n}(u^{2})=z_{n}((x-u)^{2})=0
for
n\leq-1
. In the end we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}^{(1)}(x^{2},x\cdot u,u^{2})=\frac{1}{2(x^{2}u^{2}-(x\cdot u)^{2})}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(u^{2}|x-u|(z_{n-1}((x-u)^{2})+z_{n+1}((x-u)^{2}))z_{n}(u^{2})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+|u|(u^{2}-u\cdot x)(z_{n-1}(u^{2})+z_{n+1}(u^{2}))\,{z_{n}((x-u)^{2})}\Big)\frac{C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u})}{n+1},

(112)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(x^{2},x\cdot u,u^{2})=\frac{1}{2(x^{2}u^{2}-(x\cdot u)^{2})}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(-|x-u|\,(u\cdot x)(z_{n-1}((x-u)^{2})+z_{n+1}((x-u)^{2}))z_{n}(u^{2})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+|u|(x^{2}-u\cdot x)(z_{n-1}(u^{2})+z_{n+1}(u^{2}))\,{z_{n}((x-u)^{2})}\Big)\frac{C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u})}{n+1}.

(113)

We now want to study the large-
|u|
behavior of

\mathfrak{f}^{(i)}(x^{2},x\cdot u,u^{2})
. Expanding
z_{n}(u^{2})
and

z_{n}((x-u)^{2})
for fixed
n
and large
|u|
, one finds the result in
Eq. (
86
) and

z_{n}((x-u)^{2})-z_{n}(u^{2})=\frac{\hat{u}\cdot x}{4\pi^{2}m\,(n+1)u^{2}}+{\rm O}(u^{-3}).

(114)

We note that

\frac{C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u})}{n+1}=(-1)^{n}\Big(1+n(n+2)((\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})^{2}-1)\frac{x^{2}}{6u^{2}}\Big)+{\rm O}(u^{-3}).

(115)

One then sees that
\langle(u\cdot\hat{\epsilon})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}
vanishes for
x=0
.
For this reason, we may rewrite

\displaystyle\langle(u\cdot\hat{\epsilon})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}

(116)

\displaystyle=\frac{|u|}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(z_{n-1}(u^{2})+z_{n+1}(u^{2})\Big)(-1)^{n}\Big(z_{n}((x-u)^{2})(-1)^{n}\frac{C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u})}{n+1}-z_{n}(u^{2})\Big).

The second factor in the sum is given in leading order by
\frac{\hat{u}\cdot x}{4\pi^{2}m\,(n+1)u^{2}}
for large
u
, and since the series
is then still absolutely convergent, one finds that

\langle(u\cdot\hat{\epsilon})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}
falls off at least as fast as

1/|u|^{3}
. In fact, at little more work reveals that the coefficient
of the
1/|u|^{3}
term vanishes. The same argument shows that

\langle(x\cdot\hat{\epsilon})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}

goes like
|u|^{-3}
for large
|u|
,
thus showing that
\big\langle\hat{\epsilon}_{\delta}J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}
falls off at least as fast as

|u|^{-3}
for large
|u|
.

III.7
Calculation of
t_{\alpha\beta}(x,u)

First, calculate at
x=0
:

\displaystyle t_{\alpha\beta}(0,u)=\langle(\hat{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\hat{\epsilon}_{\beta}-\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}=(u_{\alpha}u_{\beta}-\frac{u^{2}}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\;b(u^{2}),

(117)

Contracting with
u_{\alpha}u_{\beta}
and using the identity from
Eq. (
62
) and the orthogonality
relations (
63
) within the angular
average
\langle\ldots\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}
, we get

\displaystyle b(u^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3u^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\,(z_{n-2}(u^{2})+z_{n}(u^{2})(1-\delta_{n0})+z_{n+2}(u^{2}))\,z_{n}(u^{2})

(118)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3u^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\,z_{n}(u^{2})(z_{n}(u^{2})(1-\delta_{n0})+2z_{n+2}(u^{2}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|u|\to\infty}}{{\sim}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{96\pi^{2}m^{2}|u|^{4}}\Big(-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}+\frac{1}{6}\Big).

(119)

For the general case we have with
v=x-u
the decomposition:

\displaystyle t_{\alpha\beta}(x,u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(u_{\alpha}u_{\beta}-\frac{u^{2}}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}(u^{2},u\cdot v,v^{2})

(120)

\displaystyle+(v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}-\frac{v^{2}}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}(u^{2},u\cdot v,v^{2})

\displaystyle+(u_{\alpha}v_{\beta}+v_{\alpha}u_{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\alpha\beta}(u\cdot v))\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}(u^{2},u\cdot v,v^{2}).

Multiplying with
u_{\alpha}u_{\beta},v_{\alpha}v_{\beta}
and
u_{\alpha}v_{\beta}+v_{\alpha}u_{\beta}
and solving the system of three equations,
we get as intermediate result for the scalar functions
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(k)}

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}\\
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}\\
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{2D_{u,v}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c@{~~}c@{~~}c}3(v^{2})^{2}&u^{2}v^{2}+2(u\cdot v)^{2}&-3v^{2}(u\cdot v)\\
u^{2}v^{2}+2(u\cdot v)^{2}&3(u^{2})^{2}&-3u^{2}(u\cdot v)\\
-3v^{2}(u\cdot v)&-3u^{2}(u\cdot v)&u^{2}v^{2}+2(u\cdot v)^{2}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}s_{1}\\
s_{2}\\
s_{3}\end{array}\right)\qquad

with
D_{u,v}=(u^{2}v^{2}-(u\cdot v)^{2})^{2}
and

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!s_{1}=\langle((u\cdot\hat{\epsilon})^{2}-u^{2}/4)J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},v)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=\frac{u^{2}}{4}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(z_{n-2}(u^{2})+z_{n}(u^{2})(1-\delta_{n0})+z_{n+2}(u^{2})\Big)z_{n}(v^{2})\cdot\frac{C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{v})}{n+1},

(131)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!s_{2}=\langle((v\cdot\hat{\epsilon})^{2}-v^{2}/4)J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},v)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=\frac{v^{2}}{4}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}(u^{2})\Big(z_{n-2}(v^{2})+z_{n}(v^{2})(1-\delta_{n0})+z_{n+2}(v^{2})\Big)\cdot\frac{C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{v})}{n+1},

(132)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!s_{3}=\langle(2(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot u)(\hat{\epsilon}\cdot v)-\frac{1}{2}(u\cdot v))J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},v)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|u||v|\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big[(z_{n-1}(u^{2})+z_{n+1}(u^{2}))(z_{n-1}(v^{2})+z_{n+1}(v^{2}))-(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{v})z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n}(v^{2})\Big]\frac{C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{v})}{n+1}.

(133)

The point now is that
\langle(\hat{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\hat{\epsilon}_{\beta}-\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}
is of order (
1/u^{2}
) at large
u
, but with a tensor structure
proportional to
(\hat{u}_{\beta}\hat{u}_{\delta}-\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\beta\delta})
. The average over
\hat{u}
in the next step
will cancel this leading contribution, so that
T_{\alpha\beta}(x,y)

is finite.
Anticipating the numerical implementation, we remark that it can
be advantageous to subtract a term which
vanishes upon the
\hat{u}
integration and makes the integrand
fall off faster at large
u
.

The leading asymptotic behavior of the scalar functions

s_{i}(u^{2},u\cdot x,x^{2})
for
|u|\to\infty
is

s_{1}\sim s_{2}\sim-\frac{1}{2}s_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|u|\to\infty}}{{\sim}}\frac{1}{128\pi^{2}m^{2}}\,\Big(-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}+\frac{1}{6}\Big).

(134)

One also finds that, expanding in
x
around
x=0
, and for arbitrary

|u|
,

s_{1}\Big|_{x=0}=s_{2}\Big|_{x=0}=-\frac{1}{2}s_{3}\Big|_{x=0}=\frac{3}{4}u^{4}b(u^{2}).

(135)

Furthermore, one finds

s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}={\rm O}(x^{2}).

(136)

Equations (
135
) and (
136
) can be
shown without using the explicit expression of
z_{n}(u^{2})
.

IV
Direct evaluation of the final convolution integral

In this section, we treat the final convolution integral yielding the
tensors
S
,
V_{\delta}
and
T_{\beta\delta}
(see Eqs. (
97
)-(
99
))
by performing two
angular integrations analytically, while the third angular integral as
well as the integral over the modulus are left to be done numerically.
Since this straightforward method leads to some numerical difficulties
pointed out below,
our final weight functions have been computed with the alternative method
presented in section
V
.
Nevertheless, this method, which was implemented as part of Ref.
NilsThesis
,
provided important cross-checks
(discussed in section
VI
) for the final QED kernel.
The expressions obtained for the weight functions are also used as the starting point
for the multipole-expansion method of section
V
;
see the text around Eqs. (
197
–
V
).

In order to perform the angular integrations in
\int_{u}
in
Eqs. (
97
)-(
99
), we choose a coordinate system
where
x
is pointing in the direction
\hat{e}_{0}
and
y
is in the

(\hat{e}_{0},\hat{e}_{1})
plane and we introduce the angle
\beta
between
those two vectors

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|x|(1,0,0,0),

(137)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(y_{0},y_{1},0,0),\qquad y_{1}\geq 0,

(138)

\displaystyle\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cos\beta,\qquad\qquad 0\leq\beta\leq\pi,

(139)

\displaystyle y_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|y|\cos\beta,

(140)

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|y|\sin\beta.

(141)

The vector
u
is parametrized as follows

\displaystyle u_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|u|\cos\phi_{1},

\displaystyle u_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|u|\sin\phi_{1}\cos\phi_{2},

\displaystyle u_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|u|\sin\phi_{1}\sin\phi_{2}\cos\phi_{3},

\displaystyle u_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|u|\sin\phi_{1}\sin\phi_{2}\sin\phi_{3},

(142)

with
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in[0,\pi]
,
\phi_{3}\in[0,2\pi]
and the
angular integration measure is given by

\int d\Omega_{\hat{u}}=\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\sin^{2}\phi_{1}\int_{-1}^{1}d\hat{c}_{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi_{3}

(143)

where
\hat{c}_{2}\equiv\cos\phi_{2}
.

From the above definitions of the vectors we get

\displaystyle x\cdot u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|x||u|\cos\phi_{1},

(144)

\displaystyle u\cdot y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|u|(y_{0}\cos\phi_{1}+y_{1}\sin\phi_{1}\,\hat{c}_{2}),

(145)

\displaystyle(u\cdot y)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{2}(y_{0}^{2}\cos^{2}\phi_{1}+2y_{0}y_{1}\cos\phi_{1}\sin\phi_{1}\,\hat{c}_{2}+y_{1}^{2}\sin^{2}\phi_{1}\,\hat{c}_{2}^{2}),

(146)

\displaystyle(u-y)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u^{2}-2u\cdot y+y^{2}=y^{2}+u^{2}-2|u|y_{0}\cos\phi_{1}-2|u|y_{1}\sin\phi_{1}\,\hat{c}_{2}.

(147)

We will need the following angular integrals, where
f(x,u)\equiv f(x^{2},x\cdot u,u^{2})=f(x^{2},|x||u|\cos\phi_{1},u^{2})
is a generic scalar
function of the vectors
x
and
u
:

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}f(x,u)\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\int d\Omega_{\hat{u}}\,\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}\,f(x,u)

(148)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\sin^{2}\phi_{1}f(x^{2},|x||u|\cos\phi_{1},u^{2})

\displaystyle\quad\times{\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{d\hat{c}_{2}}{y^{2}+u^{2}-2|u|y_{0}\cos\phi_{1}-2|u|y_{1}\sin\phi_{1}\,\hat{c}_{2}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{2\pi|u||y|\sin\beta}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\sin\phi_{1}f(x^{2},|x||u|\cos\phi_{1},u^{2})\,{\rm Log},

where we introduced the abbreviation

{\rm Log}\equiv\ln\left[\frac{y^{2}+u^{2}-2|u||y|\cos(\beta-\phi_{1})}{y^{2}+u^{2}-2|u||y|\cos(\beta+\phi_{1})}\right].

(149)

In a similar way one obtains

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}\;(x\cdot u)f(x,u)\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{|x|}{2\pi|y|\sin\beta}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\sin\phi_{1}\cos\phi_{1}f(x^{2},|x||u|\cos\phi_{1},u^{2})\,{\rm Log},

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}\;(y\cdot u)f(x,u)\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\sin^{2}\phi_{1}f(x^{2},|x||u|\cos\phi_{1},u^{2})

(151)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(1+\frac{y^{2}+u^{2}}{4|u||y|\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}}\,{\rm Log}\right),

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{(y\cdot u)^{2}}{(u-y)^{2}}f(x,u))\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{8\pi|u||y|\sin\beta}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\sin\phi_{1}f(x^{2},|x||u|\cos\phi_{1},u^{2})

(152)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big[4|u||y|\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}\left(u^{2}+y^{2}+2|u||y|\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+(u^{2}+y^{2})^{2}\,{\rm Log}\Big].

The integration over
\phi_{3}
is always trivial and the integrations
over
\hat{c}_{2}
lead to simple elementary integrals.

IV.1
Calculation of the weight function
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}

Exploiting the behavior of
s(x,u)
for large
|u|
from
Eq. (
101
), we can introduce a fixed vector
w
and modify the integrand of
S(x,y)

in Eq. (
97
) to get an IR-regulated
function as follows

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}(|x|,x\cdot y,|y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S(x,y)_{\rm IR~reg.}\equiv\int_{u}\,\left(G_{0}(u-y)\,s(x,u)-\frac{G_{0}(u)\theta(u^{2}-w^{2})}{192\pi^{2}m^{2}u^{2}}\right)

(153)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi|y|\sin\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\;u^{2}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\sin\phi_{1}

(154)

\displaystyle\times\Big[\frac{|y|\sin\beta\;\theta(u^{2}-w^{2})}{192\pi m^{2}u^{3}}+{\rm\,Log\,}\cdot\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})\,\frac{C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u})}{n+1}\Big],

where we used the result from Eq. (
148
) for the angular
integration. The term with
\theta(u^{2}-w^{2})
in
Eq. (
153
) is independent of
x
and
y
,
therefore it will not affect the final result for the QED kernel,
in which only the derivatives of the weight function

\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}(|x|,x\cdot y,|y|)

with respect to
x_{\alpha}
and
y_{\alpha}
enter; see
Eq. (
30
), (
33
–
34
) and (
40
).

IV.2
Calculation of the weight functions
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1,2)}

Starting from the definition of
V_{\delta}(x,y)
in
Eq. (
36
), we split off the integration over the
length of the vector
u
and parametrize the angular average as
follows:

\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}\;\big\langle\hat{\epsilon}_{\delta}J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\big\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}=x_{\delta}\;\mathfrak{g}_{u}^{(1)}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2})+y_{\delta}\;\mathfrak{g}_{u}^{(2)}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2}).

(155)

From this we get the vector weight functions in
Eq. (
41
) via

\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(i)}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,u^{3}\mathfrak{g}^{(i)}_{u}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2}),\quad i=1,2.

(156)

Multiplying Eq. (
155
) by
x_{\delta}
and
y_{\delta}
we
then obtain in a similar way as before

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{g}_{u}^{(1)}\\
\mathfrak{g}_{u}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{x^{2}y^{2}-(x\cdot y)^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c@{~~}c}y^{2}&-x\cdot y\\
-x\cdot y&x^{2}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}(x^{2}\;\mathfrak{f}^{(1)}(x,u)+(x\cdot u)\;\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(x,u))\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}\\
\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}((x\cdot y)\;\mathfrak{f}^{(1)}(x,u)+(y\cdot u)\;\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(x,u))\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}\end{array}\right).

Using the results of the angular integrals from
Eqs. (
148
), (
) and (
151
), we
get the following results for the weight functions

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(2)}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2})=\frac{1}{8\pi y^{2}|x|\sin^{3}\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\;u^{2}\int_{0}^{\pi}{d\phi_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Big\{2\sin\beta+\Big(\frac{y^{2}+u^{2}}{2|u||y|}-\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1}\Big)\frac{\rm Log}{\sin\phi_{1}}\Big\}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n+1}((x-u)^{2})\Big[|x-u|\cos\phi_{1}\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}+(|u|\cos\phi_{1}-|x|)\frac{C_{n+1}}{n+2}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+z_{n+1}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})\Big[(|u|\cos\phi_{1}-|x|)\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}+|x-u|\cos\phi_{1}\frac{C_{n+1}}{n+2}\Big]\Big),

(165)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(3)}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2})=\frac{-1}{8\pi x^{2}|y|\sin\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\;u^{2}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\sin\phi_{1}\,{\rm Log}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n+1}((x-u)^{2})\Big(|x-u|\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}+|u|\frac{C_{n+1}}{n+2}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+z_{n+1}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})\Big(|u|\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}+|x-u|\frac{C_{n+1}}{n+2}\Big)\Big),

(166)

and

\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2})=\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(3)}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2})-\frac{|y|}{|x|}\cos\beta\,\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(2)}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2}).

(167)

We choose to first compute the scalar function
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(3)}
because
the integrand simplifies and is collinear safe for
\sin\phi_{1}\to 0
.
The argument of the Gegenbauer polynomials
C_{n}
and
C_{n+1}
is given
by

\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u}=-\frac{|u|-|x|\cos\phi_{1}}{|u-x|}.

(168)

We note that there is a large cancellation inside the curly bracket of
Eq. (
165
). If we call
A=2|u||y|/(u^{2}+y^{2})
, such that

0\leq A\leq 1
, then Taylor-expanding the logarithm in
A
shows that
the curly bracket is of order
A^{2}
at small
A
(small
A

corresponds to both
|u|\ll|y|
and
|u|\gg|y|
).

IV.3
Calculation of the weight functions
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(1,2,3)}

Upon integrating over the angular variables of
u
in
Eq. (
37
), the tensor decomposition reads

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}\langle(\hat{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\hat{\epsilon}_{\beta}-\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\,J(\hat{\epsilon},u)\,J(\hat{\epsilon},x-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}

\displaystyle=(x_{\alpha}x_{\beta}-\frac{x^{2}}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\;\mathfrak{l}^{(1)}_{u}+(y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}-\frac{y^{2}}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\;\mathfrak{l}^{(2)}_{u}+(x_{\alpha}y_{\beta}+y_{\alpha}x_{\beta}-\frac{x\cdot y}{2}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\;\mathfrak{l}^{(3)}_{u}.

(169)

where the scalar functions
\mathfrak{l}^{(1)}_{u}
depend on
x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2}
, as well as
on
|u|
. From this we get the tensor weight functions in
Eq. (
42
) via

\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(i)}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\;u^{3}\;\mathfrak{l}^{(i)}_{u}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2}),\qquad i=1,2,3.

(170)

Again, multiplying Eq. (
169
) with
x_{\alpha}x_{\beta}
,

y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}
and
x_{\alpha}y_{\beta}+y_{\alpha}x_{\beta}
and
solving as before, the weight functions
\mathfrak{l}^{(i)}_{u}
are
given by

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mathfrak{l}^{(1)}_{u}\\
\mathfrak{l}^{(2)}_{u}\\
\mathfrak{l}^{(3)}_{u}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{2D_{x,y}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c@{~~}c@{~~}c}3(y^{2})^{2}&x^{2}y^{2}+2(x\cdot y)^{2}&-3y^{2}(x\cdot y)\\
x^{2}y^{2}+2(x\cdot y)^{2}&3(x^{2})^{2}&-3x^{2}(x\cdot y)\\
-3y^{2}(x\cdot y)&-3x^{2}(x\cdot y)&x^{2}y^{2}+2(x\cdot y)^{2}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}v_{1,u}\\
v_{2,u}\\
v_{3,u}\end{array}\right),\quad

with
D_{x,y}=(x^{2}y^{2}-(x\cdot y)^{2})^{2}
and

\displaystyle v_{1,u}=\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}\Big\{[(u\cdot x)^{2}-u^{2}x^{2}/4]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}+[((x-u)\cdot x)^{2}-(x-u)^{2}x^{2}/4]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\qquad+[2(u\cdot x)((x-u)\cdot x)-x^{2}(u\cdot(x-u))/2]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}\Big\}\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}},\phantom{\underbrace{A}_{A}}

(181)

\displaystyle v_{2,u}=\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}\Big\{[(u\cdot y)^{2}-u^{2}y^{2}/4]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}+[((x-u)\cdot y)^{2}-y^{2}(x-u)^{2}/4]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\qquad+[2(u\cdot y)((x-u)\cdot y)-y^{2}(u\cdot(x-u))/2]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}\Big\}\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}},\phantom{\underbrace{A}_{A}}

(182)

\displaystyle v_{3,u}=\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}\Big\{[2(x\cdot u)(y\cdot u)-u^{2}(x\cdot y)/2]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\qquad+[2(x\cdot(x-u))(y\cdot(x-u))-(x\cdot y)(x-u)^{2}/2]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\qquad+[2(u\cdot x)(y\cdot(x-u))+2(u\cdot y)(x\cdot(x-u))-(u\cdot(x-u))(x\cdot y)]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}\Big\}\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}},

(183)

where the
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i)}(u^{2},u\cdot v,v^{2})
are given in
Eq. (
III.7
).

Using the results of the angular integrations in Eqs. (
148
),
(
)-(
152
), we get more explicitly
the following intermediate result

\displaystyle v_{1,u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-x^{2}}{2\pi uy\sin\beta}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\,\sin\phi_{1}\,{\rm Log}\Big\{u^{2}[\cos^{2}\phi_{1}-\frac{1}{4}]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}

(184)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+[\frac{3}{4}x^{2}-\frac{3}{2}ux\cos\phi_{1}+u^{2}(\cos^{2}\phi_{1}-\frac{1}{4})]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+[\frac{3}{2}ux\cos\phi_{1}-2u^{2}(\cos^{2}\phi_{1}-\frac{1}{4})]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}\Big\},

\displaystyle v_{2,u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-1}{8\pi uy\sin\beta}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\,\sin\phi_{1}\;\Big[4uy\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}(u^{2}+y^{2}+2uy\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1})

(185)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+(u^{2}+y^{2})^{2}\,{\rm Log}\Big]\Big\{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}+\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}-2\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}\Big\}

\displaystyle-\frac{x}{2\pi u\tan\beta}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\,\sin\phi_{1}\;\Big[4uy\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}+(u^{2}+y^{2})\,{\rm Log}\Big]\Big\{-\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}+\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}\Big\}

\displaystyle-\frac{y}{2\pi u\sin\beta}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\,\sin\phi_{1}\;{\rm Log}\ \Big\{[-u^{2}/4]\,\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+[x^{2}\cos^{2}\beta-\frac{1}{4}(x^{2}-2ux\cos\phi_{1}+u^{2})]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}-\frac{u}{2}[x\cos\phi_{1}-u]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}\Big\},

\displaystyle v_{3,u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-x}{2\pi u\tan\beta}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\,\sin\phi_{1}\;{\rm Log}\ \Big\{[-u^{2}/2]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}

(186)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+[3x^{2}/2-xu\cos\phi_{1}-u^{2}/2]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}+[u^{2}+ux\cos\phi_{1}]\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}\Big\}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi uy\sin\beta}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\;\sin\phi_{1}\Big[4uy\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}+(y^{2}+u^{2})\,{\rm Log}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big\{2xu\cos\phi_{1}\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}-2(x^{2}-xu\cos\phi_{1})\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}+2(x^{2}-2ux\cos\phi_{1})\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{(3)}\Big\}.

Similar to Eq. (
170
), we define weight functions

\bar{v}^{(i)}
after the integration of
v_{i,u}
over the length of
the vector
u
:

\bar{v}^{(i)}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\;u^{3}\;v_{i,u}(x^{2},x\cdot y,y^{2}),\qquad i=1,2,3.

(187)

Schematically, we have the following structure,

\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{l}}_{u}=M(x,y)\;N\;M(u,x-u)\;\overrightarrow{s},

(188)

where
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{l}}_{u}
,

\overrightarrow{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the components of

\overrightarrow{s}
are given by Eqs. (
131
)-(
133
),
and
M
is the symmetric
3\times 3
matrix given in
Eqs. (
III.7
) and (
IV.3
), which is a function of two
vectors. In particular,
\overrightarrow{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}}=M(u,x-u)\,\overrightarrow{s}
