. The linear operator
N
corresponds to
the relations (
184
)-(
186
),
\overrightarrow{v}_{u}=N\,\overrightarrow{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}}
.

The linear operator
M(x,y)\;N\;M(u,x-u)
leads to fairly long
algebraic expressions. We note that the final step, going from the

\overrightarrow{v}_{u}
to the
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{l}}_{u}
, is a
purely algebraic one. If there are linear combinations of the

\overrightarrow{v}_{u}
that lead to simpler integrands, those can be
used and the linear combinations can be resolved in terms of the

\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{l}}_{u}
at the end. We find that the
following linear combinations have manageable expressions
7
7

7

The
idea is that instead of directly applying the matrix
M(x,y)
on

\overrightarrow{v}_{u}
, we first triangularize the linear system:
compute
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(4)}=2xy^{2}(y\,\cos\beta\,\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}+x\;\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(3)})
and
\bar{v}^{(1)}=\frac{3}{4}x^{4}\;\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(1)}+x^{2}y^{2}(\cos^{2}\beta-\frac{1}{4})\;\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}+\frac{3}{2}x^{3}y\cos\beta\;\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(3)}
.
,

\displaystyle\!\!\bar{v}^{(1)}=\frac{-1}{4\pi y\sin\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,u^{2}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\,\sin\phi_{1}{\,\rm Log\,}\cdot\Big(s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}\Big),

(189)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(4)}\equiv\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\beta}\left(\bar{v}^{(3)}-\frac{2|y|}{|x|}\cos\beta\;\bar{v}^{(1)}\right)=\frac{1}{4\pi|x|\sin^{3}\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}du\,u\int_{0}^{\pi}{d\phi_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Big\{2\sin\beta+\Big(\frac{u^{2}+y^{2}}{2uy}-\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1}\Big)\frac{{\rm\,Log\,}}{\sin\phi_{1}}\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Big(2(u\cos\phi_{1}-x)\;s_{1}+2u\cos\phi_{1}\;s_{2}+(2u\cos\phi_{1}-x)\;s_{3}\Big),

(190)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}=-\frac{1}{64\pi x^{2}y^{5}\sin^{5}\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{du}{u^{2}}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{d\phi_{1}}{\sin^{3}\phi_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big\{\Big(3u^{4}+8u^{2}y^{2}+3y^{4}+4u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2\beta)-6uy(u^{2}+y^{2})\cos(\beta-\phi_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2(\beta-\phi_{1}))+4u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2\phi_{1})-6u^{3}y\cos(\beta+\phi_{1})-6uy^{3}\cos(\beta+\phi_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2(\beta+\phi_{1}))\Big)\,{\rm Log}\,+12uy\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}(u^{2}+y^{2}-2uy\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1})\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Big([2u^{2}+3x^{2}-6ux\cos\phi_{1}+u^{2}\cos(2\phi_{1})]\;s_{1}+u^{2}[2+\cos(2\phi_{1})]\;s_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+u[-3x\cos\phi_{1}+u(2+\cos(2\phi_{1}))]\;s_{3}\Big).

(191)

Once
\bar{v}^{(1)}
,

\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(4)}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}
have been
computed,
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(1)}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(3)}
are
recovered by taking successively the linear combinations

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2x^{2}y^{2}}\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(4)}-\frac{y}{x}\,\cos\beta\;\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}

(192)

and

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{3x^{4}}\Big(\bar{v}^{(1)}-x^{2}y^{2}(\cos^{2}\beta-\frac{1}{4})\;\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}-\frac{3}{2}x^{3}y\cos\beta\;\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(3)}\Big).

(193)

Next, we study the behavior of the integrands at large
u
. The curly
bracket in Eq. (
190
) is of order
1/u^{2}
, and the bracket
containing the
s_{i}
is at most of order
1/u
, based on the
properties (
135
)-(
136
). Therefore the
integrand in Eq. (
190
) is at most of order
1/u^{2}
and the
integral is absolutely convergent (numerically, it appears to fall off
even faster, perhaps as
1/u^{3}
). Similarly, in Eq. (
191
)
the curly bracket is of order
1/u
, and the bracket containing the

s_{i}
is at most of order unity. Therefore the integrand of
Eq. (
191
) is at most of order
1/u^{3}
and the integral is
absolutely convergent.

The case of the integrand in Eq. (
189
) is a bit more subtle.
It is helpful to consider what happens at either
x=0
or
y=0
from
the beginning. At
y=0
, starting from Eq. (
120
)
one finds that

T_{\alpha\beta}(x,0)=\frac{4}{3\pi x^{2}}~(\hat{x}_{\alpha}\hat{x}_{\beta}-\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\int_{0}^{\infty}du\;u\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\,\sin^{2}\phi_{1}\;(s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}).

(194)

Now, it is obvious that when
x=y=0
,
\int d^{4}u\;G_{0}(u)\;\langle(\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}-\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})J(\hat{\epsilon},u)J(\hat{\epsilon},-u)\rangle_{\hat{\epsilon}}
vanishes,
because
\langle u_{\alpha}u_{\beta}-\frac{1}{4}u^{2}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\rangle_{\hat{u}}=0
. How does this result emerge
from Eq. (
194
) ? We have already seen that

(s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3})
vanishes to linear order (included) in
|x|
. One
then finds that the quadratic order does not vanish, however it
vanishes upon performing the
\phi_{1}
integral in
Eq. (
194
),

\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{1}{\pi x^{2}}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\,\sin^{2}\phi_{1}\;(s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3})=0.

(195)

It turns
out that expanding the summand of the
s_{i}
in a Taylor series for
small
x
, the Taylor coefficients fall off with increasing powers of

1/u
. This would be obvious on dimensional grounds if the muon mass
did not enter the expression. On close inspection, the only factor
that could spoil this property are the factors

z_{n}(u^{2}-2|u||x|\cos\phi_{1}+x^{2})
, which are dimensionless functions
of
m\sqrt{u^{2}-2|u||x|\cos\phi_{1}+x^{2}}
. However, for large argument
these functions go like
z_{n}(u^{2})\sim\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}(n+1)m|u|}
,
so that the dependence on the mass factors out and the dimensional
argument applies; note that for this leading behavior, the sum over

n
is still absolutely convergent. The fact that the Taylor series
of
\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\,\sin^{2}\phi_{1}\;(s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3})
at small
|x|

starts at order
|x|^{3}
(at the earliest) thus implies that the
expression is at most of order
|x|^{3}/(m^{2}|u|^{3})
at large
|u|
; the
integrals over
|u|
in Eq. (
189
) and in
Eq. (
194
) is then absolutely convergent.
For the numerical implementation, one option is then to subtract the

O(x^{2})
term from
(s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3})
; this has the advantage of making
the
u
-integrand absolutely convergent in the infrared prior to the

\phi_{1}
integral.

For completeness, let us also treat the case where first
x
is set
to zero. For
x=0
, we can use Eq. (
117
), the expansion of
the massless propagator in Gegenbauer polynomials in
Eqs. (
66
) and (
67
) and the property
(
62
) to find

T_{\alpha\beta}(0,y)=\frac{1}{6}(\hat{y}_{\alpha}\hat{y}_{\beta}-\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\alpha\beta})\int_{0}^{\infty}du\;u^{5}\;b(u^{2})\,d_{2}(u^{2},y^{2}).

(196)

V
Final convolution integral via the multipole expansion of the massless propagator

While the method presented in the previous section can be used to numerically calculate the QED weight functions,
certain difficulties arise in special kinematic configurations of the vectors
x
and
y
.
Especially the regime where
x
and
y
are near-collinear can be challenging, in view of the inverse powers of
\sin\beta

present for instance in Eqs. (
165
) or
(
191
). Recall the definition
\cos\beta=\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y}
.

We therefore explore a different method to obtain the QED weight functions numerically.
The idea is to use the multipole expansion of the massless propagator to obtain the
weight functions in the form of a series of polynomials in
\cos\beta
, times a function
of
(|x|,|y|)
. The relevant polynomials, as it turns out, are either the Gegenbauer polynomials
themselves, or their derivatives.

Let
f(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})
be a smooth test function.
Directly integrating the expression
\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}f(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})
over two of the three angles parametrizing
u
yields a logarithm
(see Eq. (
148
)), which is found in the expressions for

\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}
(Eq.
153
),
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(3)}
(Eq.
166
)
and
\bar{v}^{(1)}
(Eq.
189
). As before we will introduce
\cos\phi_{1}=\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u}
. If instead one makes use of the multipole expansion of the propagator,
as well as of the completeness and orthogonality of the Gegenbauer polynomials,
one obtains for the same integral a sum over these
polynomials. Matching the two expressions leads to the result

-\frac{1}{4|u||y|\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}}\,{\rm Log}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{d_{n}(u^{2},y^{2})}{n+1}C_{n}(\cos\beta)C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1}),

(197)

which we will use in the sense of distributions, i.e. inserted in an
integral over
\phi_{1}
. The function
d_{n}(u^{2},y^{2})
has been defined
in Eq. (
67
).
Further useful results emerge from integrating the expressions
(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{y})\frac{f(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})}{(u-y)^{2}}

and
(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{y})^{2}\frac{f(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})}{(u-y)^{2}}

in the two different ways described above, thus leading to the
equalities

\displaystyle S_{1}\equiv-\frac{1}{4|u||y|\sin\beta}\Big(2\sin\beta+\Big(\frac{u^{2}+y^{2}}{2|u||y|}-\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1}\Big)\,\frac{\rm Log}{\sin\phi_{1}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sin^{2}\beta\;\sin^{2}\phi_{1}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{n}(u^{2},y^{2})\,C_{n}^{\,\prime}(\cos\beta)\;C_{n}^{\,\prime}(\cos\phi_{1})}{n(n+1)(n+2)},

(198)

\displaystyle S_{2}\equiv-\frac{1}{8u^{3}y^{3}\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}}\cdot

\displaystyle\Big\{\Big(3u^{4}+8u^{2}y^{2}+3y^{4}+4u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2\beta)-6uy(u^{2}+y^{2})\cos(\beta-\phi_{1})+u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2(\beta-\phi_{1}))

\displaystyle+4u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2\phi_{1})-6u^{3}y\cos(\beta+\phi_{1})-6uy^{3}\cos(\beta+\phi_{1})+u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2(\beta+\phi_{1}))\Big)\,{\rm Log}\,

\displaystyle+12uy\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}(u^{2}+y^{2}-2uy\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1})\Big\}

\displaystyle=4\sin^{4}\beta\;\sin^{4}\phi_{1}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\frac{d_{n}(u^{2},y^{2})\,C_{n}^{\,\prime\prime}(\cos\beta)C_{n}^{\,\prime\prime}(\cos\phi_{1})}{(n-1)n(n+1)(n+2)(n+3)}.

(199)

Equality (
V
) show that two powers of
\sin^{2}\beta
can be extracted
explicitly from the angular integrals
for
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(2)}
(Eq.
165
) and
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(4)}
(Eq.
190
).
Similarly, Eq. (
V
) shows that four powers of
\sin^{2}\beta
can be extracted
from the angular integral for
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}
(Eq.
191
),
allowing one to cancel analytically otherwise numerically problematic inverse powers of
\sin\beta
. In
this way, the case where
x
and
y
are exactly collinear can be
calculated directly, without the use of an extrapolation to

\sin\beta=0
. The price one pays for this cancellation is that
the sum over the derivatives of the Gegenbauer polynomials converges somewhat
less rapidly: for instance,
C_{n}(1)=n+1
, while
C_{n}^{\,\prime}(1)=\frac{n}{3}(n+1)(n+2)
.

V.1
Derivation of Eqs. (
197
)-(
V
)

Let
f(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})
be a smooth test function. As given in Eq. (
148
), explicit integration over
the spherical-coordinate angles
\phi_{2}
and
\phi_{3}
yields

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}\;f(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}=-\frac{1}{4|u||y|\sin\beta}\Big\langle\frac{{\rm Log}}{\sin\phi_{1}}\cdot f(\cos\phi_{1})\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}.

(200)

On the other hand, using the expansion of the massless propagator in
Eq. (
66
) and of the function
f
in Gegenbauer
polynomials in Eq. (
64
),
as well as the orthogonality property
(
63
), one finds

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{1}{(u-y)^{2}}\;f(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d_{n}(u^{2},y^{2})\;\frac{C_{n}(\cos\beta)}{n+1}\;\langle C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1})f(\cos\phi_{1})\rangle_{\hat{u}}.

(201)

Comparing the expressions yields Eq. (
197
).

In the same way, consider the two treatments of the following angular average,

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{\hat{u}\cdot\hat{y}-(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y})(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})}{(u-y)^{2}}\;f(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4|u||y|\sin\beta}\Big\langle\Big(2\sin\beta+(\frac{u^{2}+y^{2}}{2|u||y|}-\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1})\,\frac{\rm Log}{\sin\phi_{1}}\Big)f(\cos\phi_{1})\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{C_{n}(\cos\beta)}{n+1}\,\Big\langle C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1})\Big(\frac{1}{2}(d_{n+1}+d_{n-1})-\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1}d_{n}\Big)f(\cos\phi_{1})\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}.

(202)

We are using the convention
d_{n}\equiv 0
for
n<0
and the argument
of the
d_{n}
coefficients is
(u^{2},y^{2})
throughout this subsection.
Comparing the two equations yields an expression for
S_{1}
,

S_{1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{C_{n}(\cos\beta)}{n+1}C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1})\Big(\frac{1}{2}(d_{n+1}+d_{n-1})-\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1}d_{n}\Big).

(203)

We can now manipulate the sum in the following way. First apply the
cosine factors on the Gegenbauer polynomials, using
Eq. (
61
),

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n+1}\Big({C_{n}(\cos\beta)}C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1})\frac{1}{2}(d_{n+1}+d_{n-1})

(204)

\displaystyle-\frac{d_{n}}{4}(C_{n+1}(\cos\beta)+C_{n-1}(\cos\beta))(C_{n+1}(\cos\phi_{1})+C_{n-1}(\cos\phi_{1}))\Big)

Next, shift the summation index in the second term so as to factorize
C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1})
, and then collect the
terms multiplying
d_{n+1}
and those multiplying
d_{n-1}
,

\displaystyle S_{1}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1})}{n+1}\Big[\frac{d_{n+1}}{n+2}\Big((n+3)C_{n}(\cos\beta)-(n+1)C_{n+2}(\cos\beta)\Big)

(205)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{d_{n-1}}{n}\Big((n-1)C_{n}(\cos\beta)-(n+1)C_{n-2}(\cos\beta)\Big)\Big].

Note that
\frac{d_{n-1}}{n}
should be interpreted as zero for
n=0
.
Now we notice that the combination appearing in the brackets can be expressed through the derivative of
the Gegenbauer polynomials,

(n+2)C_{n-1}(z)-nC_{n+1}(z)=2(1-z^{2})C_{n}^{\,\prime}(z),\qquad\qquad n\geq 0.

(206)

Then shifting again the summation index so as to factorize
C_{n}^{\,\prime}(\cos\beta)
,
we arrive at

S_{1}=\frac{1-\cos^{2}\beta}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}d_{n}\,\frac{C_{n}^{\,\prime}(\cos\beta)}{n+1}\Big(\frac{C_{n-1}(\cos\phi_{1})}{n}-\frac{C_{n+1}(\cos\phi_{1})}{n+2}\Big)

(207)

(we have used the fact that
C_{n=0}^{\,\prime}(\cos\beta)=0
to drop the
n=0
term);
and finally, identifying again the expression for the derivative of
C_{n}
in the bracket, we obtain Eq. (
V
).

Thirdly, one finds by the same method as above

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{y})^{2}}{(u-y)^{2}}\;f(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{16u^{3}y^{3}\sin\beta}\Big\langle\Big(4uy\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}(u^{2}+y^{2}+{2uy}\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1})+(u^{2}+y^{2})^{2}{\rm Log}\Big)\frac{f(\cos\phi_{1})}{\sin\phi_{1}}\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{C_{n}(\cos\beta)}{n+1}(d_{n-2}+(2-\delta_{n0})d_{n}+d_{n+2})\Big\langle C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1})\;f(\cos\phi_{1})\Big\rangle_{\hat{u}},

(208)

which provides us with a new identity.
The specific linear combination appearing in the QED weight function

\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}
in Eq. (
191
) is

\displaystyle S_{2}\equiv-\frac{1}{8u^{3}y^{3}\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}}\cdot

\displaystyle\Big\{\Big(3u^{4}+8u^{2}y^{2}+3y^{4}+4u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2\beta)-6uy(u^{2}+y^{2})\cos(\beta-\phi_{1})+u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2(\beta-\phi_{1}))

\displaystyle+4u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2\phi_{1})-6u^{3}y\cos(\beta+\phi_{1})-6uy^{3}\cos(\beta+\phi_{1})+u^{2}y^{2}\cos(2(\beta+\phi_{1}))\Big)\,{\rm Log}\,

\displaystyle+12uy\sin\beta\sin\phi_{1}(u^{2}+y^{2}-2uy\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1})\Big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{C_{n}(\cos\beta)}{n+1}C_{n}(\cos\phi_{1})\Big[2d_{n}\Big(\frac{1}{2}+\cos(2\beta)+\cos(2\phi_{1})+\frac{1}{2}\cos(2\beta)\cos(2\phi_{1})\Big)

\displaystyle-6\cos\beta\cos\phi_{1}(d_{n+1}+d_{n-1})+\frac{3}{2}(d_{n+2}+(2-\delta_{n0})d_{n}+d_{n-2})\Big],

(209)

where we have used Eqs. (
197
), (
V
) and (
208
) in obtaining the second equality.
Manipulations on expression (
209
) similar to those yielding Eq. (
V
), including the use of

\displaystyle-2\Big((n+3)(n-1)+3\delta_{n0}\Big)C_{n}(z)+(n+2)(n+3)C_{n-2}(z)+n(n-1)C_{n+2}(z)

\displaystyle=4(1-z^{2})^{2}C_{n}^{\,\prime\prime}(z),\qquad n\geq 0,

(210)

lead to Eq. (
V
).

V.2
Gegenbauer expansion of the QED weight functions

Using the relations above,
we provide alternative expressions to compute the six required weight
functions and their derivatives.
Using Eq. (
197
), one derives the following representation
for three of the weight functions with argument
(|x|,\hat{c}_{\beta},|y|)
, where
\hat{c}_{\beta}=\cos\beta
,

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}\\
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(3)}\\
\bar{v}^{(1)}\end{array}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}C_{n}(\hat{c}_{\beta})\left[\frac{1}{|y|^{n+2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha^{(0)}_{n-}\\
\alpha^{(3)}_{n-}\\
\alpha^{(1)}_{n-}\end{array}\right)+|y|^{n}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha^{(0)}_{n+}\\
\alpha^{(3)}_{n+}\\
\alpha^{(1)}_{n+}\end{array}\right)\right].

(211)

Here the coefficients
\alpha^{(k)}_{n\pm}
, for
k=0,1,3
, have argument
(|x|,|y|)
, and their functional form is

\displaystyle\alpha^{(k)}_{n-}(|x|,|y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k3}}{\pi(n+1)}\int_{0}^{|y|}\!\!\!u^{n+3}du\int_{0}^{\pi}\!d{\phi_{1}}\,\hat{s}_{1}^{2}\;C_{n}(\hat{c}_{1})\;\sigma_{k}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|),\qquad

(212)

\displaystyle\alpha^{(k)}_{n+}(|x|,|y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k3}}{\pi(n+1)}\int_{|y|}^{\infty}\frac{du}{u^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{\pi}d{\phi_{1}}\,\hat{s}_{1}^{2}\;C_{n}(\hat{c}_{1})\;\sigma_{k}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|),

(213)

where
\hat{c}_{1}=\cos\phi_{1}
and
\hat{s}_{1}=\sin\phi_{1}
.
The functions
\sigma_{k}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|)
appearing in the coefficients
\alpha^{(k=0,1,3)}_{n\pm}
,
as well as those appearing in
\beta^{(k=2,4)}_{n\pm}
and

\gamma^{(2)}_{n\pm}
below,
are given explicitly at the end of this subsection, Eqs. (
220
)-(
227
).
As a remark, we have already noted that
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}
contains a logarithmic infrared divergence.
In the present representation, that divergence is entirely contained in the coefficient
\alpha^{(0)}_{0+}
,
which makes a constant contribution to
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}
, independent of
x
and
y
. Since only derivatives
of
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}
with respect to
x
or
y
appear in the QED kernel,
the coefficient
\alpha^{(0)}_{0+}
is never actually needed.

Next, starting from Eqs. (
165
) and (
190
),
and using Eq. (
V
), one obtains the representation

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(2)}\\
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(4)}/y^{2}\end{array}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}C_{n}^{\,\prime}(\hat{c}_{\beta})\left[\frac{1}{|y|^{n+3}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta^{(2)}_{n-}\\
\beta^{(4)}_{n-}\end{array}\right)+|y|^{n-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta^{(2)}_{n+}\\
\beta^{(4)}_{n+}\end{array}\right)\right].

(214)

with (for
k=2,4
)

\displaystyle\beta^{(k)}_{n-}(|x|,|y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k2})}{\pi\,n(n+1)(n+2)}\int_{0}^{|y|}du\,u^{n+3}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\;\hat{s}^{2}_{1}\;C_{n}^{\,\prime}(\hat{c}_{1})\;\sigma_{k}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|),\qquad\quad

(215)

\displaystyle\beta^{(k)}_{n+}(|x|,|y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k2})}{\pi\,n(n+1)(n+2)}\int_{|y|}^{\infty}\frac{du}{u^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\;\hat{s}^{2}_{1}\;C_{n}^{\,\prime}(\hat{c}_{1})\;\sigma_{k}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|).

(216)

Finally, using Eqs. (
191
) and (
V
), we obtain the form

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}(|x|,\hat{c}_{\beta},|y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}C_{n}^{\,\prime\prime}(\hat{c}_{\beta})\Big(\frac{1}{|y|^{n+4}}\gamma^{(2)}_{n-}(|x|,|y|)+|y|^{n-2}\gamma^{(2)}_{n+}(|x|,|y|)\Big),

(217)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{n-}(|x|,|y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\,(n-1)\,n\,(n+1)(n+2)(n+3)}\cdot

(218)

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{0}^{|y|}du\;u^{n+3}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\;\hat{s}^{2}_{1}\;C_{n}^{\,\prime\prime}(\hat{c}_{1})\;\sigma_{5}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|)

\displaystyle\gamma^{(2)}_{n+}(|x|,|y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\,(n-1)\,n\,(n+1)(n+2)(n+3)}\cdot

(219)

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{|y|}^{\infty}\frac{du}{u^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\phi_{1}\;\hat{s}^{2}_{1}\;C_{n}^{\,\prime\prime}(\hat{c}_{1})\;\sigma_{5}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|).

We now give the explicit expressions for the sums
\sigma_{k}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|)
,
k=0,\dots,5
.
They involve the modified Bessel functions and the Gegenbauer polynomials. The argument of the
C_{n}
and
C_{n}^{\,\prime}

polynomials is always
(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u})
; this is only indicated explicitly in the relatively compact expression for
\sigma_{0}
,
which in fact coincides with
s(x,u)
.
For the weight functions expanded in
C_{n}(\hat{c}_{\beta})
in
Eq. (
211
), the sums are

\displaystyle\sigma_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}(u^{2})\,z_{n}((x-u)^{2})\,\frac{C_{n}(\hat{u}\cdot\widehat{x-u})}{n+1},

(220)

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big\{z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n+1}((x-u)^{2})\Big[|x-u|\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}+|u|\frac{C_{n+1}}{n+2}\Big]

(221)

\displaystyle\qquad+z_{n+1}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})\Big[|u|\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}+|x-u|\frac{C_{n+1}}{n+2}\Big]\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|x|}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big\{\frac{z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n+1}((x-u)^{2})}{n+2}\Big[\frac{|x|\hat{s}^{2}_{1}}{|x-u|}\,\frac{C_{n+1}^{\,\prime}}{n+1}+\hat{c}_{1}C_{n+1}\Big]

(222)

\displaystyle+\frac{z_{n+1}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})}{n+1}\Big[-\frac{|x|\hat{s}^{2}_{1}}{|x-u|}\frac{C_{n}^{\,\prime}}{n+2}+\hat{c}_{1}C_{n}\Big]\Big\},

and

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}\equiv s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}=-\frac{x^{2}}{4}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}

(223)

\displaystyle+\frac{u^{2}}{4}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{n}((x-u)^{2})\Big(z_{n-2}(u^{2})+(2-\delta_{n0})z_{n}(u^{2})+z_{n+2}(u^{2})\Big)\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}

\displaystyle+\frac{(x-u)^{2}}{4}\sum_{n\geq 0}z_{n}(u^{2})\Big(z_{n-2}((x-u)^{2})+(2-\delta_{n0})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})+z_{n+2}((x-u)^{2})\Big)\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}

\displaystyle+\frac{|u||x-u|}{4}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big\{\Big(z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n+2}((x-u)^{2})+2z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})+z_{n+2}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})\Big)\frac{C_{n+1}}{n+2}

\displaystyle+\Big(z_{n-2}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})+2z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})+z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n-2}((x-u)^{2})\Big)\frac{C_{n-1}}{n}\Big\}.

For the weight functions expanded in
C_{n}^{\,\prime}(\hat{c}_{\beta})
in
Eq. (
214
), the sums are

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|x|}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big\{z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n+1}((x-u)^{2})\Big[|x-u|\hat{c}_{1}\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}+(|u|\hat{c}_{1}-|x|)\frac{C_{n+1}}{n+2}\Big]\qquad

(224)

\displaystyle+z_{n+1}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})\Big[(|u|\hat{c}_{1}-|x|)\frac{C_{n}}{n+1}+|x-u|\hat{c}_{1}\frac{C_{n+1}}{n+2}\Big]\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{s}^{2}_{1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big\{\frac{z_{n}(u^{2})z_{n+1}((x-u)^{2})}{n+2}\Big[\frac{|x|\hat{c}_{1}}{|x-u|}\frac{C_{n+1}^{\,\prime}}{n+1}-C_{n+1}\Big]

(225)

\displaystyle-\frac{z_{n+1}(u^{2})z_{n}((x-u)^{2})}{n+1}\Big[\frac{|x|\hat{c}_{1}}{|x-u|}\,\frac{C_{n}^{\,\prime}}{n+2}+C_{n}\Big]\Big\}.

and

\displaystyle\sigma_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|u|}\Big((2\frac{|u|}{|x|}\hat{c}_{1}-1)\sigma_{1}+s_{2}-s_{1}\Big).

(226)

Finally, the sum appearing in
\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}
is

\displaystyle\sigma_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{u^{2}}\Big(\frac{|u|}{x^{2}}(|u|(1+2\hat{c}_{1}^{2})-3|x|\hat{c}_{1})\sigma_{1}+3\frac{|u|}{|x|}\hat{c}_{1}(s_{2}-s_{1})+3s_{1}\Big).

(227)

The sums
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are evaluated as indicated in Eqs. (
131
) and (
132
).

For numerical purposes, it is usually preferable to evaluate the derivative of an
integrand with respect to a parameter before the integral is performed numerically.
Therefore, for completeness, we provide in appendix
B
the expressions
of the
|x|
-derivative of the sums defining the weight functions, namely
the
\{\sigma_{k}\}_{k=0}^{5}
as well as
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
.
In the expressions provided in appendix, the first two derivatives of the functions
z_{n}(u^{2})
appear.
Thanks to Eq. (
87
), they can be computed practically in an iterative fashion as follows,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial z_{n}}{\partial|u|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n+1}{4\pi^{2}mu^{2}}\,{K_{0}(mu)I_{n+1}(m|u|)}-\frac{z_{n}(u)}{|u|},

(228)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}z_{n}}{\partial|u|^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{u}\Big(\frac{\partial z_{n}}{\partial|u|}\Big)+\frac{n(n+2)}{u^{2}}\,z_{n}(u)-\frac{n+1}{2\pi^{2}u^{2}}\,K_{1}(m|u|)I_{n+1}(m|u|).

(229)

Appendix
C
provides the relevant expressions to obtain the QED weight functions and the full kernel
at
x=0
or at
y=0
. The motivation for investigating these special cases is twofold.
First, the expressions simplify compared to the general case in that they have one fewer integral or infinite sum.
Thus, their evaluation is significantly faster and provides a cross-check for the numerics of the general case,
which should approach the special cases in the appropriate limits.
Second, when we consider modifications of the QED kernel via subtractions in section
VIII.1
,
the kernel at
x=0
or
y=0
will be needed explicitly.
The QED kernel for
y=x
can be obtained from the case
y=0
using the property (
22
).

VI
Numerical evaluation of the QED kernel

The basic idea of our approach is to precompute and store the weight
functions, since by O(4) symmetry they are functions of three
variables. This stands in stark contrast with the QED kernel itself,
which is a function of eight real variables and has 384 independent
components. Since up to two derivatives with respect to components of
x
and
y
act
on the tensors
S(x,y)
,
V_{\delta}(x,y)
and
T_{\beta\delta}(x,y)
, chain rules are used
to convert these derivatives, when they act on the weight functions,
into derivatives with respect to the variables
(|x|,\,\hat{c}_{\beta}\equiv\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y},\,|y|)
,
for instance

\partial_{\alpha}^{(x)}=\hat{x}_{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial|x|}+\frac{1}{|x|}(\hat{y}_{\alpha}-\hat{c}_{\beta}\hat{x}_{\alpha})\frac{\partial}{\partial\hat{c}_{\beta}}.

(230)

The chain-rule based expressions for the tensors
T^{\rm A}_{\alpha\beta\delta}(x,y)
in terms of
the weight functions are given in appendix
A
(
A=\;
I, II, III).
With these rank-three tensors at hand, and with the Dirac traces
{\cal G}^{A}
computed upon initialization,
the QED kernel is obtained via a simple linear combination, Eq. (
30
).

We have pursued two strategies to numerically compute the
QED weight function. The first is based on Eqs. (
154
,

165
,
166
,
189
,
190
,

191
), followed by taking the appropriate linear
combinations. In this strategy, the weight functions are computed on a
three-dimensional grid, each direction representing one of the
variables
|x|
,
\hat{c}_{\beta}
and
|y|
.
While we will not describe this implementation in detail (see
NilsThesis
for more information),
it is worth mentioning that the logarithm appearing in each of the six equations
referenced above required a dedicated treatment in the regions where its argument vanishes.
The second strategy,
which is the one we opted for in our subsequent tests and lattice QCD
calculations, consists in calculating the coefficients of the
weight-function expansion in Gegenbauer polynomials according to
Eqs. (
211
,
214
,
217
). The
coefficients are functions of
|x|
and
|y|
and carry an index
corresponding to the order of the polynomial in
\hat{c}_{\beta}

which they multiply. Implementing both strategies with two
independent codes allowed us to have a valuable cross-check of our
results.
In the following, we describe a sample of the results
obtained with the second strategy and the most important technical
aspects involved in the numerical calculation.
It is worth mentioning at this point the order-of-magnitude computational cost
of precomputing the required weight functions provided at
KQEDcode
:
it amounted in total to about three weeks on a dual-core laptop.

The three tensor weight functions as well as the derivatives of the
scalar weight function appearing in the kernel
\bar{\cal L}_{[\rho\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)
for given values of
|x|
and

\hat{c}_{\beta}
are displayed in
Fig.
4
. The result of the numerical integration is
shown as a curve. At
y=0
, we confront the numerical results with
the Taylor expansion of the weight functions obtained in appendix

C
and observe good agreement
8
8

8

For

\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(2)}
, we did not derive a prediction at
y=0

because it is not needed for the QED kernel.
.
In addition, the large-
|y|
asymptotics for the
derivatives of the scalar function
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}
are determined
in appendix
D
and displayed for
m|y|>5
in
the three left panels of Fig.
4
.
Similarly, Fig.
5
shows the two vector weight functions.
The scalar and tensor weight functions have unit of
{\rm GeV}^{-2}
, while
the vector ones have unit of
{\rm GeV}^{-1}
. It is natural to use the muon mass
to build dimensionless combinations. We note that
all weight functions are smooth functions of
|y|
, and that they have
rather different magnitudes in units of the muon mass. The scalar and the first vector weight
function are largest, the other weight functions being at least an
order of magnitude smaller. We have found this hierarchy to be fairly
generic. For the reader’s convenience, we have collected a few numerical values
of the weight functions in Table
1
.
Quantitative checks have been performed against the weight functions resulting from the first computational
strategy described in the previous paragraph.
For instance, at the reference point
m_{\mu}|x|=0.436
,
\hat{c}_{\beta}=-0.59375
and
m_{\mu}|y|=0.654
,
all derivatives required for the QED kernel have been compared; with
m_{\mu}
set to unity,
the largest absolute difference was found in

\frac{\partial^{2}g_{1}}{\partial|x|\partial\hat{c}_{\beta}}
, and amounted to
7\times 10^{-9}
.

We next describe some of the numerical techniques we have used to
arrive at the results presented in Fig.
4
and

5
. We first note that we have worked in double precision throughout,
and have not found it necessary to employ further enhanced arithmetic precision.
In the representation of Eqs. (
211
,
214
,
217
) of the weight functions, one
has to carry out a two-dimensional integral of an integrand which is
represented as an infinite sum over products of modified Bessel
functions and a Gegenbauer polynomial or its derivatives. One
integration variable represents the angle between the position vectors

x
and
u
, the other is the norm of
u
. The most important
numerical task is thus to evaluate efficiently a sum involving the
modified Bessel functions and the Gegenbauer polynomial or its derivatives.

We have evaluated strings of modified Bessel functions (e.g.
K_{0}
,

K_{1}
, …,
K_{n_{\rm max}}
) using routines inspired by those given
in
NR
. The most important aspect is that the modified Bessel
functions of the second kind (
K_{n}
) can be evaluated using the
recursion relation among them in the direction of increasing index

n
, while those of the first kind (
I_{n}
) must be evaluated in a
downward recursion, starting from a sufficiently large
n
. Since we
need these functions for a wide range of
n
, we store them on the
fly during the recursion.

Next, we use the Clenshaw algorithm (see for instance
NR
) to perform the sum,
exploiting the recursion relations

\displaystyle C_{n+1}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2zC_{n}(z)-C_{n-1}(z)\qquad\qquad\qquad(n\geq 1),

(231)

\displaystyle C_{n+1}^{\,\prime}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2z\,\frac{n+1}{n}C_{n}^{\,\prime}(z)-\frac{n+2}{n}C_{n-1}^{\,\prime}(z)\qquad(n\geq 1),

(232)

\displaystyle C_{n+1}^{\,\prime\prime}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2z\,\frac{n+1}{n-1}C_{n}^{\,\prime\prime}(z)-\frac{n+3}{n-1}C_{n-1}^{\,\prime\prime}(z)\qquad(n\geq 2)

(233)

among the polynomials. Thus none of the
C_{n}
,
C_{n}^{\,\prime}
and

C_{n}^{\,\prime\prime}
are evaluated explicitly in the calculation of the sums.

We have performed the integration using the integrator
cubature

cubature
. This integrator is able to perform numerical
integrals on a multi-dimensional rectangular region. For calculating
the weight functions, we have mostly used the
p
-adaptive cubature
routine, which uses a tensor product of Clenshaw-Curtis quadrature
rules; the degree of the rules is doubled along each dimension until
convergence is achieved. An advantage of the
cubature
package is
that it allows for a vector of integrands. Since it is the different
coefficients
\alpha_{m}^{(k)}(|x|,|y|)
(
k=0,1,3
),

\beta_{m}^{(k)}(|x|,|y|)
(
j=2,4
) and
\gamma^{(2)}_{m}(|x|,|y|)
that
are being calculated, and that they all involve the same sums (one of
the
\sigma_{i}
(
i=0,\dots,5
)), only the Gegenbauer polynomial

C_{m}(\hat{u}\cdot\hat{x})
(or its derivative) must be reevaluated for
different values of the index
m
. This saves a significant amount of
computations. Because the Gegenbauer polynomial is strongly
oscillating for large
m
, the calculation of the large-order
coefficients to a given relative precision dominates the computing
time.

We have turned the integral over
|u|
from
|y|
to
\infty
into an
integral from
0
to
1/|y|
by making the change of variables
u_{1}=1/|u|
. Given that we want to compute the coefficients of the Gegenbauer-polynomial expansion for all
|y|
,
a considerable amount of computing time is saved by simply observing that

\displaystyle\alpha^{(k)}_{m-}(|x|,|y|+\Delta|y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{(k)}_{m-}(|x|,|y|)

(234)

\displaystyle+\frac{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k3}}{\pi(m+1)}\int_{|y|}^{|y|+\Delta|y|}\!\!\!u^{m+3}du\int_{0}^{\pi}\!d{\phi_{1}}\,\hat{s}_{1}^{2}\;C_{m}(\hat{c}_{1})\;\sigma_{k}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|),

\displaystyle\alpha^{(k)}_{m+}(|x|,|y|-\Delta|y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha^{(k)}_{m+}(|x|,|y|)

(235)

\displaystyle+\frac{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k3}}{\pi(m+1)}\int_{|y|-\Delta|y|}^{|y|}\frac{du}{u^{m-1}}\int_{0}^{\pi}d{\phi_{1}}\,\hat{s}_{1}^{2}\;C_{m}(\hat{c}_{1})\;\sigma_{k}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|),

and similarly for the other coefficients. In this way, the cost of
increasing the resolution
1/\Delta|y|
with which the coefficients
are computed is very low. We also recall that the derivative of the coefficients
with respect to
|y|
are needed to obtain the QED kernel.
We have used the fact that one can easily obtain these
|y|
-derivatives analytically at practically zero
computational cost. Indeed, since

\frac{1}{|y|^{m+2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial|y|}\alpha^{(k)}_{m-}(|x|,|y|)+|y|^{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial|y|}\alpha^{(k)}_{m+}(|x|,|y|)=0,

(236)

we have for instance

\frac{\partial}{\partial|y|}\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}=\sum_{m\geq 0}C_{m}(\hat{c}_{\beta})\left[-(m+2)\frac{1}{|y|^{m+3}}\alpha^{(0)}_{m-}+m|y|^{m-1}\alpha^{(0)}_{m+}\right],

(237)

and similar expressions apply to all six weight functions. The derivatives with respect to
\hat{c}_{\beta}

are of course simply obtained by analytically deriving the Gegenbauer polynomial.
Finally, the
|x|
dependence of the weight functions appears only in the sums
\sigma_{k}(|x|,\hat{c}_{1},|u|)
.
Their first
|x|
-derivative is given analytically in Appendix
B
.
Only the second
|x|
-derivative of the sums
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}

was computed numerically by taking a finite difference (with step size
m\delta|x|=10^{-3}
) of the analytically obtained first derivative.

Figure 4:

\bar{\mathfrak{g}}^{(0)}
appearing

and

.

\bar{\mathfrak{l}}^{(1)}
,

.

(|y|^{0})

; note that the latter weight function is multiplied by a tensor of order
|y|^{2}

in the QED kernel.

Table 1:
The weight functions (multiplied by

) for
|x|=0.4
,

and
