. The muon mass is set to unity throughout this table.

An important question in the method based on the series in Gegenbauer polynomials is, how
many terms are needed to reach a good approximation to the weight function.
The answer obviously depends on
|x|
and
|y|
. Consider the case of the scalar weight function,
which is given by the integral of the massless propagator
G_{0}(y-u)
multiplied with
s(x,u)
, for fixed vectors
x
and
y
.
If one used the expansion of
s(x,u)
in
C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})
, together with the
expansion of
G_{0}(y-u)
in
C_{n}(\hat{y}\cdot\hat{u})
, the result of the angular integration (see Eq. (
63
))
would be to give the expansion of the scalar weight function in
C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y})
, with coefficients
proportional to the product of the coefficients in the two series appearing in the integrand.
The expansion of the scalar weight function in
C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y})
is precisely the series whose coefficients we compute numerically.
Thus, for that series to converge rapidly, it is sufficient that for all
u
, of the multipole expansion of the massless propagator
and the expansion of
s(x,u)
in
C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})
, at least one converges rapidly.
The multipole expansion of
G_{0}(y-u)
converges poorly when
|u|=|y|
;
the expansion of
s(x,u)
in
C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{u})
converges poorly when
|u|=|x|
.
Thus to guarantee that the series in
C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y})
converges rapidly, one must avoid the case
|x|\approx|y|
.
The latter condition however defines a surface of codimension one in the space of
(x,y)
, and the poor convergence thus affects
a substantial fraction of the sampled
(x,y)
points. However, we can use the symmetry (
43
) to compute the weight function with
the argument
y
exchanged for
(x-y)
, which in general will improve the convergence of the series in
C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y})
,
since the value of
|x|
is then substantially different from
|x-y|
. The only case where the convergence cannot be improved by using
the symmetry property of the weight function is when the points
(0,x,y)
form an equilateral triangle. The condition for this to happen
however is a subset of codimension two. Thus if the convergence of the series cannot be controlled in a region
||x|-|y||<\Delta
and

||x|-|x-y||<\Delta
, an integral over the QED kernel with a function which is smooth for an equilateral-triangle constellation of
(0,x,y)

will suffer an error of order
\Delta^{2}
. To guarantee an accurate computation of the QED kernel in the equilateral-triangle constellation,
additional computations would be required. In the practical applications of the QED kernel, we have not found it necessary to
improve further the quality of its evaluation for an overall accuracy on
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
on the order of one percent.
The considerations above apply to all weight functions, and the symmetry relations (
43
-
48
) can be exploited
to always compute the weight functions with the most favorable arguments for the purpose of the expansion in Gegenbauer polynomials.

Some trial-and-error was necessary to find out an appropriate extent
and step size
9
9

9

The required extent of the grid is dictated by the physics entering the correlation
function
i\widehat{\Pi}
; it was chosen large enough for the
\pi^{0}
exchange
to be reproduced with subpercent precision at
m_{\pi}=135\,
MeV. The step size should be small enough
that the achieved precision on the grid points does not get ‘spoiled’ entirely by the interpolation. Since
the weight functions vary more rapidly at small arguments
|x|
and
|y|
, a relatively small step size
was chosen in this region.
for the grid in
|x|
and
|y|
.
Both variables were sampled up to
|x|_{\rm max}=|y|_{\rm max}=6.54m^{-1}
. For the
variable
m|y|
, which is cheap to sample finely, we use a step size
of
\delta_{\rm g}=0.0242222
. For the variable
m|x|
, we use the same
fine step size for small
m|x|
up to
m|x|=0.363333
, and a step size
three times bigger for the larger
m|x|
. The number of coefficients
of the Gegenbauer-polynomial expansion computed for a given pair

(|x|,|y|)
was chosen
10
10

10

The stability of the resulting weight functions was tested
by varying the number of terms in the Gegenbauer-polynomial series.
to be
(8+{\rm floor}(5m|x|))
.
For
m|y|<\delta_{\rm g}
, we use the Taylor-expansion given in
appendix
C
in order to interpolate the QED kernel
between the point at
m|y|=0
and the first point of the grid at
m|y|=\delta_{\rm g}
. We proceed similarly in the case
m|x|<\delta_{\rm g}
. The
large-
|y|
asymptotics derived in appendix
D

were not used in the numerical implementation, they only served
to acquire a qualitative understanding of the large-distance behavior
of the QED kernel.

As sketched at the beginning of this section,
given precomputed coefficients of the Gegenbauer polynomial expansion of the weight functions on a grid in
|x|
and
|y|
,
the remaining computational tasks to obtain the QED kernel at a given
(x,y)
point are: to evaluate the sums yielding
the weight functions (again using the Clenshaw algorithm), interpolate
the weight functions (and their derivatives), apply the chain rules to obtain the
T^{({\rm A})}_{\alpha\beta\delta}
tensors and
finally to perform the contraction of these with the
{\cal G}^{\rm A}_{\delta[\rho,\sigma]\mu\alpha\nu\beta\lambda}
tensors.
We have written a code in the
C
programming language to perform these tasks.
Some implementation details and a link to the code can be found in appendix
E
.

VII
Example calculations of the four-point amplitude
i\widehat{\Pi}

In this section we derive explicit expressions for

i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)
in several models that
are relevant for understanding the corresponding tensor in QCD. Our
primary goal is to use these four-point functions to test (in
section
VIII
) the validity of the coordinate-space
approach to
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
developed here. In addition, we will
gain insight into the shape and range of the integrand, information
which is valuable in preparing the lattice-QCD calculation.

But first, we review the most important properties of the tensor

i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)
.

VII.1
General properties of
i\widehat{\Pi}

The rank-five tensor has the Bose symmetries

\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\nu\mu\lambda\sigma}(y,x)=\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\lambda\nu\mu\sigma}(-x,y-x).

(238)

Combining the two generators of Bose symmetries from Eq. (
238
), we obtain a finite symmetry group with
3!
elements.
Note that while the first equality in Eq. (
238
) follows immediately from the definition of
\widehat{\Pi}
,
the second one requires translation symmetry and the property that

\int d^{4}z\,\langle j_{\mu}(x)j_{\nu}(y)j_{\sigma}(z)j_{\lambda}(0)\rangle=0,

(239)

which holds in infinite volume as a consequence of the observation
that a conserved current can be written as a total divergence,

j_{\sigma}(z)=\partial^{(z)}_{\alpha}(z_{\sigma}j_{\alpha}(z)),

(240)

and Gauss’s theorem.

The tensor
\widehat{\Pi}
has the further discrete symmetry

\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(-x,-y)=-\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)

(241)

as a consequence of the space and Euclidean-time reversal symmetries of QCD.
Current conservation implies

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}^{(x)}\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=0,

(242)

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu}^{(y)}\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=0,

(243)

\displaystyle(\partial_{\lambda}^{(x)}+\partial_{\lambda}^{(y)})\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=0.

(244)

For the last result, we have again assumed infinite volume and translation symmetry, and made use of Eq. (
239
).
For the same reasons, we have the properties

\int_{x}\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=\int_{y}\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=0,

(245)

which will be exploited in the numerical tests of section
VIII.1
.
These properties hold in the continuum formulation of QED or QCD. In a
theory like scalar QED (see subsection
VII.4
below), the
appropriate contact terms, as predicted by the Ward identities of
current conservation, must be included into the definition of

i\widehat{\Pi}
in order for Eq. (
245
) to be
satisfied.

We finally give the representation of
i\widehat{\Pi}
in terms of the Euclidean momentum-space HLbL amplitude.
The latter is related to the position-space four-point function of the
electromagnetic current via a triple Fourier transform,

\Big\langle j_{\mu}(x)j_{\nu}(y)j_{\sigma}(z)j_{\lambda}(0)\Big\rangle=\int_{q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}}e^{i(q_{1}\cdot x+q_{2}\cdot y+q_{3}\cdot z)}\Pi_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}).

(246)

The quantity of interest,
i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)
, can then be calculated using the equation

i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=-i\int_{q_{1},q_{2}}e^{i(q_{1}\cdot x+q_{2}\cdot y)}\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{3\rho}}\Pi_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\Big|_{q_{3}=0}.

(247)

This relation is particularly useful when form factors are introduced to describe the coupling of mesons to photons,
as in the case of the pion-pole contribution.

VII.2
Pion-pole contribution to hadronic light-by-light
scattering in the VMD model

As a starting point for calculating the contribution of the pion pole, we take the Minkowski-space
expression for the HLbL amplitude given in
KN_02
. To convert it to Euclidean space, we use the
prescription given in
Gerardin:2017ryf
, whereby we arrive at

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\Big|_{\pi_{0}}=\frac{{\cal F}(-q_{1}^{2},-q_{2}^{2}){\cal F}(-q_{3}^{2},-(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})^{2})}{(q_{1}+q_{2})^{2}+m_{\pi}^{2}}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\,q_{1\alpha}\,q_{2\beta}\;\epsilon_{\sigma\lambda\gamma\delta}\,q_{3\gamma}\,(q_{1}+q_{2})_{\delta}

\displaystyle+\frac{{\cal F}(-q_{1}^{2},-(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})^{2}){\cal F}(-q_{2}^{2},-q_{3}^{2})}{(q_{2}+q_{3})^{2}+m_{\pi}^{2}}\epsilon_{\mu\lambda\alpha\beta}\,q_{1\alpha}\,(q_{2}+q_{3})_{\beta}\;\epsilon_{\nu\sigma\gamma\delta}\,q_{2\gamma}\,q_{3\delta}

\displaystyle+\frac{{\cal F}(-q_{1}^{2},-q_{3}^{2}){\cal F}(-q_{2}^{2},-(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3})^{2})}{(q_{1}+q_{3})^{2}+m_{\pi}^{2}}\epsilon_{\mu\sigma\alpha\beta}\,q_{1\alpha}\,q_{3\beta}\;\epsilon_{\nu\lambda\gamma\delta}\,q_{2\gamma}\,(q_{1}+q_{3})_{\delta}.

(248)

From here the required derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{3\rho}}\Pi_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})|_{q_{3}=0}

is obtained straightforwardly. Before Fourier transforming it with respect to
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
,
we choose a specific parametrization of the form factor.

VII.2.1
Vector-meson dominance parametrization of the form factor

In the following, we use the vector-meson dominance (VMD) model for the
transition form factor,

{\cal F}(-q_{1}^{2},-q_{2}^{2})=\frac{c_{\pi}}{(q_{1}^{2}+m_{V}^{2})(q_{2}^{2}+m_{V}^{2})},\qquad

(249)

its normalization
c_{\pi}=-\frac{N_{c}m_{V}^{4}}{12\pi^{2}F_{\pi}}
being determined by the axial anomaly.
Inserting this form into the expression for
\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{3\rho}}\Pi_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})|_{q_{3}=0}
,
and the latter into (
247
), and
rewriting the expression using coordinate-space propagators, one finds the master expression

\displaystyle i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=\frac{c_{\pi}^{2}}{m_{V}^{2}(m_{V}^{2}-m_{\pi}^{2})}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\alpha}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\beta}}\Big\{\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\epsilon_{\sigma\lambda\rho\gamma}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\gamma}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\gamma}}\Big)K_{\pi}(x,y)

\displaystyle+\epsilon_{\mu\lambda\alpha\beta}\epsilon_{\nu\sigma\gamma\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\gamma}}K_{\pi}(y-x,y)+\epsilon_{\mu\sigma\alpha\rho}\epsilon_{\nu\lambda\beta\gamma}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\gamma}}K_{\pi}(x,x-y)\Big\},

(250)

where

K_{\pi}(x,y)\equiv\int_{u}\Big(G_{m_{\pi}}(u)-G_{m_{V}}(u)\Big)G_{m_{V}}(x-u)G_{m_{V}}(y-u)=K_{\pi}(y,x).

(251)

We remind the reader that we denote by
G_{M}
the scalar propagator with mass
M
; see Eq. (
52
).

Thus the main task is to compute the scalar function
K_{\pi}(x,y)
, which depends on three scalar quantities,

|x|
,
\cos\beta\equiv\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y}
and
|y|
.
The three derivatives that must be applied onto the function
K_{\pi}(x,y)
are computed using a lengthy chain rule.

The goal is therefore to compute
K_{\pi}(x,y)
and the derivatives with respect to the scalar variables
analytically as far as possible.
Using the expansion of the scalar propagator in Gegenbauer polynomials and exploiting their orthogonality property,
one obtains immediately an expansion of
K_{\pi}(x,y)
in
C_{n}(\hat{x}\cdot\hat{y})
.
Via the change of integration variable
u\to x-u
in Eq. (
251
) and the subsequent
Gegenbauer expansion of the propagators, one arrives at the alternative expansion

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 0}C_{n}(\widehat{x-y}\cdot\hat{x})\;b_{n}(|x|,|x-y|),

(252)

\displaystyle b_{n}(|x|,|x-y|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi^{2}}{n+1}\int_{0}^{\infty}d|u|\;|u|^{3}G_{m_{V}}(u)\;\delta\gamma_{n}(x^{2},u^{2})\;\gamma_{n}((x-y)^{2},u^{2}),

(253)

with
\delta\gamma_{n}(x^{2},u^{2})=\gamma_{n}(x^{2},u^{2})|_{m_{\pi}}-\gamma_{n}(x^{2},u^{2})|_{m_{V}}
the difference of the expansion coefficients of two
massive propagators with different masses in Gegenbauer
polynomials; see Eq. (
69
) for the explicit
expression for
\gamma_{n}(x^{2},u^{2})
. Because the difference of two
propagators is only logarithmically divergent at the origin, the
convergence of the multipole expansion is improved. A further,
analogous expression for
K_{\pi}(x,y)
expanded in

C_{n}(\widehat{x-y}\cdot\hat{y})
is obtained by interchanging
x
and

y
on the right-hand side of Eq. (
252
).

An important consideration in the evaluation of
K_{\pi}(x,y)
, very
similar to the discussion in the penultimate paragraph of section
VI
, is the
following. For the sum in Eq. (
252
) to be rapidly
convergent, at least one of the
\delta\gamma_{n}(x^{2},u^{2})
and

\gamma_{n}((x-y)^{2},u^{2})
should decrease rapidly with
n
, for all
u
.
This works when the ratio of
|x|
and
|x-y|
is not too close to
unity. If this is not the case, the expansion in

C_{n}(\widehat{x-y}\cdot\hat{y})
can be used. The only problematic
constellation is when the points
(0,x,y)
form an equilateral
triangle; in this case, a more sophisticated technique would be
required. However, in an integral over
x
at fixed
y
for instance,
this case represents a set of codimension 2. In practice, this means
that if the sum, truncated at
n_{\rm max}
, does not provide an
accurate estimate of
K_{\pi}(x,y)
in a range
|x|=|y|(1\pm\epsilon)

and
\cos\beta=\frac{\pi}{3}(1\pm\epsilon)
, where
\epsilon
shrinks when
n_{\rm max}
increases,
the error on the resulting integral is of order
\epsilon^{2}
, since the integrand is
regular in the equilateral constellation. In our numerical implementation,
we chose
n_{\rm max}=64
.

Explicitly, the form of the coefficients is

\displaystyle b_{n}(|x|,\Delta)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta\leq|x|}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{(n+1)m_{V}}{8\pi^{4}|x|\Delta}

(254)

\displaystyle\Big\{K_{n+1}(m_{V}\Delta)\Big(K_{n+1}(m_{\pi}|x|)G^{1}_{n}(m_{\pi},m_{V},\Delta)-K_{n+1}(m_{V}|x|)G^{1}_{n}(m_{V},m_{V},\Delta)\Big)

\displaystyle+I_{n+1}(m_{V}\Delta)\Big(K_{n+1}(m_{\pi}|x|)(G^{2}_{n}(m_{\pi},m_{V},\Delta)-G^{2}_{n}(m_{\pi},m_{V},|x|))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad-K_{n+1}(m_{V}|x|)(G^{2}_{n}(m_{V},m_{V},\Delta)-G^{2}_{n}(m_{V},m_{V},|x|))\Big)

\displaystyle+I_{n+1}(m_{V}\Delta)\Big(I_{n+1}(m_{\pi}|x|)G^{3}_{n}(m_{\pi},m_{V},|x|)-I_{n+1}(m_{V}|x|)G^{3}_{n}(m_{V},m_{V},|x|)\Big)\Big\},

\displaystyle b_{n}(|x|,\Delta)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle|x|\leq\Delta}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{(n+1)m_{V}}{8\pi^{4}|x|\Delta}

(255)

\displaystyle\Big\{K_{n+1}(m_{V}\Delta)\Big(K_{n+1}(m_{\pi}|x|)G^{1}_{n}(m_{\pi},m_{V},|x|)-K_{n+1}(m_{V}|x|)G^{1}_{n}(m_{V},m_{V},|x|)\Big)

\displaystyle+K_{n+1}(m_{V}\Delta)\Big(I_{n+1}(m_{\pi}|x|)(G^{2}_{n}(m_{V},m_{\pi},|x|)-G^{2}_{n}(m_{V},m_{\pi},\Delta))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad-I_{n+1}(m_{V}|x|)(G^{2}_{n}(m_{V},m_{V},|x|)-G^{2}_{n}(m_{V},m_{V},\Delta))\Big)

\displaystyle+I_{n+1}(m_{V}\Delta)\Big(I_{n+1}(m_{\pi}|x|)G^{3}_{n}(m_{\pi},m_{V},\Delta)-I_{n+1}(m_{V}|x|)G^{3}_{n}(m_{V},m_{V},\Delta)\Big)\Big\}

with

\displaystyle G^{1}_{n}(m_{1},m_{2},|x|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{|x|}du\;K_{1}(m_{V}u)I_{n+1}(m_{1}u)I_{n+1}(m_{2}u),

(256)

\displaystyle G^{2}_{n}(m_{1},m_{2},|x|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{|x|}^{s}du\;K_{1}(m_{V}u)I_{n+1}(m_{1}u)K_{n+1}(m_{2}u),

(257)

\displaystyle G^{3}_{n}(m_{1},m_{2},|x|)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{|x|}^{\infty}du\;K_{1}(m_{V}u)K_{n+1}(m_{1}u)K_{n+1}(m_{2}u).

(258)

As a reminder,
I_{n}
and
K_{n}
denote the modified Bessel functions.
Note that the dependence of
G^{2}_{n}
on the upper limit
s
of integration drops out in the functions
b_{n}
.
In our approach to the numerical implementation,
the values of the functions
G^{1,2,3}_{n}
are computed and stored on a regular one-dimensional grid.
Then, when the function
K_{\pi}(x,y)
and its derivatives are needed at a certain target point,
the more favorable expansion in
C_{n}(\widehat{x-y}\cdot\hat{x})
and
C_{n}(\widehat{x-y}\cdot\hat{y})
is chosen,
and an interpolation is performed in the variables
|x|
and
|x-y|
, at the target value of

\cos\alpha=\widehat{x-y}\cdot\hat{x}
. A chain rule relates the derivatives with respect to
one of the variables
(|x|,\cos\beta,|y|)
to those with respect to one of the variables
(|x|,\cos\alpha,|x-y|)
.
With up to three derivatives involved, the chain rule is best generated with a symbolic manipulation
program.
A further element used in the evaluation of the derivatives is based on the observation

(-\triangle^{(y)}+M_{V}^{2})K_{\pi}(x,y)=G_{m_{V}}(x-y)\Big(G_{m_{\pi}}(y)-G_{m_{V}}(y)\Big).

(259)

This equation is used to express the second and higher derivatives with respect to
|y|
in terms
of the derivatives with respect to
\cos\beta
of the same order and in terms of lower derivatives.

VII.2.2
Tests performed

From the momentum-space expression

K_{\pi}(x,y)=(m_{V}^{2}-m_{\pi}^{2})\int_{q_{1},q_{2}}\frac{e^{i(q_{1}\cdot x+q_{2}\cdot y)}}{[(q_{1}+q_{2})^{2}+m_{\pi}^{2}](q_{1}^{2}+m_{V}^{2})(q_{2}^{2}+m_{V}^{2})[(q_{1}+q_{2})^{2}+m_{V}^{2}]},

(260)

one easily obtains

\displaystyle\int_{x,y}K_{\pi}(x,y)=\frac{m_{V}^{2}-m_{\pi}^{2}}{m_{\pi}^{2}m_{V}^{6}},

(261)

which provides a test of the numerical implementation of

K_{\pi}(x,y)
.
More differential information can also be obtained,

\displaystyle\int_{y}K_{\pi}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m_{V}^{2}}\Big(\frac{1}{m_{V}^{2}-m_{\pi}^{2}}(G_{m_{\pi}}(x)-G_{m_{V}}(x))-\frac{K_{0}(m_{V}|x|)}{8\pi^{2}}\Big).

(262)

Also, using integration by parts and the result (
261
), one shows that

\displaystyle\int_{x,y}\delta_{\mu\nu}x_{\lambda}(x_{\rho}y_{\sigma}-y_{\rho}x_{\sigma})i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=-\int_{x,y}\delta_{\mu\nu}y_{\lambda}(x_{\rho}y_{\sigma}-y_{\rho}x_{\sigma})i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{x,y}(\delta_{\nu\lambda}x_{\mu}-\delta_{\mu\lambda}y_{\nu})(x_{\rho}y_{\sigma}-y_{\rho}x_{\sigma})i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=\frac{3}{\pi^{4}m_{\pi}^{2}F_{\pi}^{2}}.

(263)

The three integrals test respectively the third, second and first term of Eq. (
250
),
which contains three terms in total.

VII.3
Lepton-loop contribution to light-by-light scattering in QED

In this subsection, we present the perturbative calculation of the
fermion loop contribution to
i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)
.

VII.3.1
The coordinate-space four-point function of the electromagnetic current

We start out by writing out the expression for the quark-connected contribution of the unintegrated coordinate-space four-point function
of the electromagnetic current, valid in an arbitrary U(
N_{c}
) gauge field background.
We note the important property

S(x,y)=\gamma_{5}S(y,x)^{\dagger}\gamma_{5}

(264)

of the fermion propagator, where the dagger acts on the Dirac indices. This property holds in an arbitrary background gauge field.

Performing the six fully-connected Wick contractions, we note that
due to the property (
264
) they pair up, corresponding to
the fermion number flowing in opposite directions. One obtains

\langle j_{\mu}(x)\;j_{\nu}(y)\;j_{\sigma}(z)\;j_{\lambda}(0)\rangle=-2{\rm Re}\{I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)+I^{(2)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)+I^{(3)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)\},

(265)

with

\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}S(x,y)\gamma_{\nu}S(y,z)\gamma_{\sigma}S(z,w)\gamma_{\lambda}S(w,x)\},

(266)

\displaystyle I^{(2)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}S(x,y)\gamma_{\nu}S(y,w)\gamma_{\lambda}S(w,z)\gamma_{\sigma}S(z,x)\}=I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y,w,z),

(267)

\displaystyle I^{(3)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\lambda}S(w,y)\gamma_{\nu}S(y,z)\gamma_{\sigma}S(z,x)\gamma_{\mu}S(x,w)\}=I^{(1)}_{\lambda\nu\sigma\mu}(w,y,z,x).

(268)

VII.3.2
Vanishing background gauge field: the QED case

Recall the free Dirac fermion propagator
S(x,y)
in Euclidean position space,

\displaystyle S(w+x,w)\equiv\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\;\frac{-ip_{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}+m}{p^{2}+m^{2}}\;e^{ip\cdot x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}}{4\pi^{2}|x|}\Big[\gamma_{\mu}x_{\mu}\,\frac{K_{2}(m|x|)}{|x|}+K_{1}(m|x|)\Big],~~~~

(269)

with

\displaystyle S(w+x,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{x_{\mu}\,\gamma_{\mu}}{2\pi^{2}(x^{2})^{2}}\qquad\qquad(m=0)

(270)

in the massless case. In the free theory, the propagator is actually Hermitian
with respect to the Dirac indices, so that the general property (
264
) holds even without the dagger.
From here on, the expressions in this section assume a single fermion flavor with unit electric charge.
Thus the calculation can be interpreted as a treatment of the contribution of a lepton of mass
m

to light-by-light scattering in
a_{\mu}
.
Also, in a vanishing background field, we note the translation-invariance property

I^{(j)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,w)=I^{(j)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x-w,y-w,z-w,0),\qquad j=1,2,3,

(271)

which can be combined with the Bose symmetry of the photons to write

\displaystyle I^{(2)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)=I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y,0,z)=I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x-z,y-z,-z,0),

(272)

\displaystyle I^{(3)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)=I^{(1)}_{\lambda\nu\sigma\mu}(0,y,z,x)=I^{(1)}_{\lambda\nu\sigma\mu}(-x,y-x,z-x,0).

(273)

Furthermore, the expression inside the curly bracket in Eq. (
265
) is already real.
In the free massless case, one thus obtains

\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(-x_{\alpha})(x-y)_{\beta}(y-z)_{\gamma}z_{\delta}}{(2\pi^{2})^{4}|x|^{4}|x-y|^{4}|y-z|^{4}|z|^{4}}{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\},

(274)

\displaystyle I^{(2)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{y_{\alpha}(-z_{\beta})(z-x)_{\gamma}(x-y)_{\delta}}{(2\pi^{2})^{4}|y|^{4}|z|^{4}|x-z|^{4}|x-y|^{4}}{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\lambda}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\delta}\},\phantom{\Bigg()}

(275)

\displaystyle I^{(3)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(y-z)_{\alpha}(z-x)_{\beta}\;x_{\gamma}\;(-y_{\delta})}{(2\pi^{2})^{4}|y-z|^{4}|z-x|^{4}|x|^{4}|y|^{4}}{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\lambda}\gamma_{\delta}\}.

(276)

In the free massive case, the result is

\displaystyle I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)=\Big(\frac{m}{2\pi}\Big)^{8}\Big[

\displaystyle\frac{(-x_{\alpha})(x-y)_{\beta}(y-z)_{\gamma}z_{\delta}\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)K_{2}(m|y-z|)K_{2}(m|z|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y|^{2}|y-z|^{2}|z|^{2}}\cdot

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}\phantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(277)

\displaystyle+\frac{K_{1}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)K_{1}(m|y-z|)K_{1}(m|z|)}{|x||x-y||y-z||z|}{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-x_{\alpha})(x-y)_{\beta}\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)K_{1}(m|y-z|)K_{1}(m|z|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y|^{2}|y-z||z|}{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-x_{\alpha})(y-z)_{\gamma}\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)K_{2}(m|y-z|)K_{1}(m|z|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y||y-z|^{2}|z|}{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-x_{\alpha})z_{\delta}\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)K_{1}(m|y-z|)K_{2}(m|z|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y||y-z||z|^{2}}{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(x-y)_{\beta}(y-z)_{\gamma}\;K_{1}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)K_{2}(m|y-z|)K_{1}(m|z|)}{|x||x-y|^{2}|y-z|^{2}|z|}{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(x-y)_{\beta}z_{\delta}\;K_{1}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)K_{1}(m|y-z|)K_{2}(m|z|)}{|x||x-y|^{2}|y-z||z|^{2}}{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(y-z)_{\gamma}z_{\delta}\;K_{1}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)K_{2}(m|y-z|)K_{2}(m|z|)}{|x||x-y||y-z|^{2}|z|^{2}}{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}\Big].

To evaluate the coordinate-space four-point function,
it is thus sufficient to program the function
I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)
,
and call it three times to compute the four-point function
\langle j_{\mu}(x)\;j_{\nu}(y)\;j_{\sigma}(z)\;j_{\lambda}(0)\rangle
.
As for the Dirac traces, it is straightforward to compute and store the 65536 components of

{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

once for all times.

VII.3.3
Calculation of
i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)

From Eq. (
265
), in order to compute

i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)=2\int_{z}z_{\rho}\;{\rm Re}\{I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)+I^{(2)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)+I^{(3)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)\},

(278)

it is sufficient to compute the two integrals

\displaystyle\widehat{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 2\,{\rm Re}\int d^{4}z\;z_{\rho}\;I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0),

(279)

\displaystyle\Pi^{(r,1)}_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 2\,{\rm Re}\int d^{4}z\;I_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}^{(1)}(x,y,z,0).

(280)

Indeed, for the second term, we make use of the property

I^{(2)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)=I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y,0,z)=I_{\nu\lambda\sigma\mu}^{(1)}(y,0,z,x)=I^{(1)}_{\nu\lambda\sigma\mu}(y-x,-x,z-x,0)

(281)

(where we have performed a cyclic permutation of the arguments in the second equality),
from which there follows

\displaystyle 2\int d^{4}z\;z_{\rho}\,I_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}^{(2)}(x,y,z,0)=2\int d^{4}z\;(z_{\rho}+x_{\rho})I^{(1)}_{\nu\lambda\sigma\mu}(y-x,-x,z,0)

(282)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\rho;\nu\lambda\mu\sigma}(y-x,-x)+x_{\rho}\,\Pi^{(r,1)}_{\nu\lambda\mu\sigma}(y-x,-x).

Similarly, the third term can be expressed as

I^{(3)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)=I^{(1)}_{\lambda\nu\sigma\mu}(0,y,z,x)=I^{(1)}_{\lambda\nu\sigma\mu}(-x,y-x,z-x,0),

(283)

so that

\displaystyle 2\int d^{4}z\;z_{\rho}\,I^{(3)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)=2\int d^{4}z\;(z_{\rho}+x_{\rho})I^{(1)}_{\lambda\nu\sigma\mu}(-x,y-x,z,0)

(284)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\rho;\lambda\nu\mu\sigma}(-x,y-x)+x_{\rho}\,\Pi^{(r,1)}_{\lambda\nu\mu\sigma}(-x,y-x).

Thus
i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)
can be expressed through the functions

\widehat{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)
and
\Pi^{(r,1)}_{\nu\lambda\mu\sigma}(x,y)
via

\displaystyle i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)

(285)

\displaystyle+\widehat{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\rho;\nu\lambda\mu\sigma}(y-x,-x)+x_{\rho}\,\Pi^{(r,1)}_{\nu\lambda\mu\sigma}(y-x,-x)

\displaystyle+\widehat{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\rho;\lambda\nu\mu\sigma}(-x,y-x)+x_{\rho}\,\Pi^{(r,1)}_{\lambda\nu\mu\sigma}(-x,y-x).

It remains to perform the required integrals. The result is

\displaystyle\widehat{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)

(286)

\displaystyle=2\Big(\frac{m}{2\pi}\Big)^{8}\Big[\frac{(-x_{\alpha})(x-y)_{\beta}K_{2}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y|^{2}}\cdot{f}_{\rho\delta\gamma}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{K_{1}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)}{|x||x-y|}\cdot{f}_{\rho\delta\gamma}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{K_{1}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)}{|x||x-y|}\,g_{\rho}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-x_{\alpha})(x-y)_{\beta}\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y|^{2}}g_{\rho}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-x_{\alpha})\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y|}h_{\rho\gamma}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(x-y)_{\beta}\;K_{1}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)}{|x||x-y|^{2}}h_{\rho\gamma}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-x_{\alpha})\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y|}\hat{f}_{\rho\delta}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(x-y)_{\beta}\;K_{1}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)}{|x||x-y|^{2}}\hat{f}_{\rho\delta}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}\Big]

(287)

and

\displaystyle\Pi^{(r,1)}_{\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)

(288)

\displaystyle=2\Big(\frac{m}{2\pi}\Big)^{8}\Big[\frac{(-x_{\alpha})(x-y)_{\beta}\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y|^{2}}\cdot l_{\gamma\delta}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{K_{1}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)}{|x||x-y|}\cdot p(|y|)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-x_{\alpha})(x-y)_{\beta}\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y|^{2}}\cdot p(|y|)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-x_{\alpha})\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y|}\cdot q_{\gamma}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(x-y)_{\beta}\;K_{1}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)}{|x||x-y|^{2}}\cdot q_{\gamma}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-x_{\alpha})\;K_{2}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)}{|x|^{2}|x-y|}\cdot q_{\delta}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\alpha}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{(x-y)_{\beta}\;K_{1}(m|x|)K_{2}(m|x-y|)}{|x||x-y|^{2}}\cdot q_{\delta}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\beta}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{K_{1}(m|x|)K_{1}(m|x-y|)}{|x||x-y|}\cdot l_{\gamma\delta}(y)\cdot{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\gamma_{\gamma}\gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\delta}\gamma_{\lambda}\}\Big].

The functions appearing in the expressions above are

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\rho\delta}(y)\equiv\int_{z}z_{\rho}z_{\delta}\frac{K_{1}(m|y-z|)}{|y-z|}\,\frac{K_{2}(m|z|)}{|z|^{2}}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{m^{3}}\Big\{\hat{y}_{\rho}\hat{y}_{\delta}\;m|y|K_{1}(m|y|)+\delta_{\rho\delta}K_{0}(m|y|)\Big\},\qquad

(289)

\displaystyle{f}_{\rho\delta\gamma}(y)\equiv\int_{z}z_{\rho}\,z_{\delta}\,(y-z)_{\gamma}\frac{K_{2}(m|y-z|)}{|y-z|^{2}}\,\frac{K_{2}(m|z|)}{|z|^{2}}=-\frac{1}{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\gamma}}\hat{f}_{\rho\delta}(y)

(290)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{m^{3}}\Big\{\hat{y}_{\gamma}\hat{y}_{\delta}\hat{y}_{\rho}\,m|y|K_{2}(m|y|)+(\delta_{\rho\delta}\hat{y}_{\gamma}-\delta_{\gamma\rho}\hat{y}_{\delta}-\delta_{\gamma\delta}\hat{y}_{\rho})\,K_{1}(m|y|)\Big\},

(291)

\displaystyle g_{\rho}(y)\equiv\int d^{4}z\;z_{\rho}\frac{K_{1}(m|y-z|)}{|y-z|}\;\frac{K_{1}(m|z|)}{|z|}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{m^{2}}y_{\rho}\,K_{0}(m|y|),

(292)

\displaystyle h_{\rho\gamma}(y)\equiv\int d^{4}z\;z_{\rho}\,(y-z)_{\gamma}\,\frac{K_{2}(m|y-z|)}{|y-z|^{2}}\,\frac{K_{1}(m|z|)}{|z|}=-\frac{1}{m}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\gamma}}g_{\rho}(y)

(293)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{m^{3}}\Big(\hat{y}_{\gamma}\hat{y}_{\rho}\,m|y|\,K_{1}(m|y|)-\delta_{\gamma\rho}K_{0}(m|y|)\Big),

(294)

\displaystyle l_{\gamma\delta}(y)\equiv\int d^{4}z\;z_{\delta}(y-z)_{\gamma}\frac{K_{2}(m|y-z|)K_{2}(m|z|)}{|y-z|^{2}|z|^{2}}

(295)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{m^{2}}\Big(\hat{y}_{\gamma}\hat{y}_{\delta}\,K_{2}(m|y|)-\delta_{\gamma\delta}\,\frac{K_{1}(m|y|)}{m|y|}\Big),

(296)

\displaystyle p(|y|)\equiv\int d^{4}z\;\frac{K_{1}(m|y-z|)}{|y-z|}\frac{K_{1}(m|z|)}{|z|}=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{m^{2}}\,K_{0}(m|y|),

(297)

\displaystyle q_{\gamma}(y)\equiv\int d^{4}z\;(y-z)_{\gamma}\frac{K_{2}(m|y-z|)}{|y-z|^{2}}\,\frac{K_{1}(m|z|)}{|z|}=-\frac{y_{\gamma}}{m|y|}p^{\prime}(|y|)=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{m^{2}}\,\hat{y}_{\gamma}\,K_{1}(m|y|).\qquad

(298)

The integrals are performed by using the Gegenbauer expansion of the massive scalar propagator, Eq. (
68
).
Then, in the case of
p(|y|)
, which is proportional to the convolution of two scalar propagators,
one makes use of the integrals
Prudnikov
11
11

11

In Eq. (
299
),
{\rm Re}(\lambda)>-1
is assumed
and in Eq. (
300
),
{\rm Re}(m)>0
is assumed. The prime denotes the derivatives of the Bessel function
with respect to their argument, e.g.
K_{0}^{\prime}(mr)=-K_{1}(mr)
.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}dz\,zK_{\lambda}(mz)\,I_{\lambda}(mz)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}}{2}\Big[\Big(1+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{2}r^{2}}\Big)I_{\lambda}(mr)K_{\lambda}(mr)-I_{\lambda}^{\prime}(mr)K_{\lambda}^{\prime}(mr)\Big]-\frac{\lambda}{2m^{2}},\qquad

(299)

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}dz\,zK_{\lambda}(mz)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}}{2}(K_{\lambda}^{\prime}(mr))^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Big(r^{2}+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{m^{2}}\Big)K_{\lambda}(mr)^{2}.

(300)

In other radial integrals, one can first reduce the order of the Bessel functions using integration by parts.

VII.4
Pion-loop contribution to light-by-light scattering in scalar QED

In this subsection we present in some detail the calculation of the charged-pion-loop contribution
to
i\widehat{\Pi}
in the framework of scalar QED.
In this framework, the pions are approximated as point particles; it should be noted that
the absence of form factors associated with the
\gamma\pi\pi
vertex leads to an

a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
contribution almost three times larger than the dispersively evaluated “pion box”
(see Ref.
Colangelo:2017fiz
).

Since some expressions are quite long and only one mass appears in the entire calculation,
we denote the pion propagator simply by
G
rather than
G_{m_{\pi}}
.
Also, the position-space vectors of the four vertices of the light-by-light amplitude
will generally be denoted by
(X^{1},X^{2},X^{3},X^{4})
, rather than
(x,y,z,0)
,
in order to notationally exploit the high degree of permutation symmetry of the amplitude.

The Euclidean Lagrangian for a massive complex scalar field minimally coupled to an external gauge field is

{\cal L}=(\partial_{\mu}+ieA_{\mu})\phi^{*}(\partial_{\mu}-ieA_{\mu})\phi+m_{\pi}^{2}\phi^{*}\phi.

(301)

It is convenient to introduce the generating functional

Z[A_{\mu}]=\int D\phi D\phi^{*}\;e^{-S[\phi,\phi^{*},A_{\mu}]}.

(302)

We want to compute the connected four-point function of the gauge field
A_{\mu}(x)
,

\tilde{\Pi}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}(X^{1},X^{2},X^{3},X^{4})\equiv\left.\frac{\delta^{4}\log Z}{\delta A_{\mu_{1}}(X^{1})\delta A_{\mu_{2}}(X^{2})\delta A_{\mu_{3}}(X^{3})\delta A_{\mu_{4}}(X^{4})}\right|_{A_{\mu}=0}.

(303)

Its relation to the desired function
i\widehat{\Pi}
is given below in Eq. (
323
).

Let

j_{\mu}(x)=i(\phi^{*}\partial_{\mu}\phi-\phi\partial_{\mu}\phi^{*})

(304)

be the electromagnetic current (in units of
e
) associated with the complex scalar field
\phi
.
We split up the calculation of
\tilde{\Pi}
into three contributions,

\tilde{\Pi}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})=\sum_{n=0,1,2}\tilde{\Pi}^{(n)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3}),

(305)

where
\tilde{\Pi}^{(n)}
is the contribution to
\tilde{\Pi}
resulting from
n
insertions of the
Lagrangian term
\Delta{\cal L}=e^{2}\phi^{*}\phi A_{\mu}A_{\mu}
and
(4-2n)
insertions
of the electromagnetic current.

VII.4.1
Four-point function of the current

As the main contribution to the four-point function of the gauge field
A_{\mu}(x)
,
we compute the four-point function of the electromagnetic current

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}^{(0)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}(X^{1},X^{2},X^{3},X^{4})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\Big\langle j_{\mu_{1}}(X^{1})j_{\mu_{2}}(X^{2})j_{\mu_{3}}(X^{3})j_{\mu_{4}}(X^{4})\Big\rangle

(306)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\rm A}={\rm I,II,III}}\tilde{\Pi}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}^{(0),{\rm A}}(X^{1},X^{2},X^{3},X^{4}).

(307)

There are 16 individual four-point functions generated by the two terms of each current. Each one gives rise to 6 Wick contractions.
Thus there are 96 Wick contractions in total.

The three types of terms that we distinguish in Eq. (
307
)
differ by the number of derivatives acting on
\phi
and on the
\phi^{*}
respectively,

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}^{(0),{\rm I}}(X^{1},X^{2},X^{3},X^{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\langle\prod_{k=1}^{4}\phi(X^{k})^{*}\partial_{\mu_{k}}\phi(X^{k})\Big\rangle+\Big\langle\textrm{herm. conjug.}\Big\rangle,

(308)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}^{(0),{\rm II}}(X^{1},X^{2},X^{3},X^{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{l=1}^{4}\Big\{\Big\langle\phi(X^{l})\partial_{\mu_{l}}\phi(X^{l})^{*}\;\prod_{k\neq l}^{4}\phi(X^{k})^{*}\partial_{\mu_{k}}\phi(X^{k})\Big\rangle

(309)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\Big\langle\textrm{herm. conjug.}\Big\rangle\Big\},

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}^{(0),{\rm III}}(X^{1},X^{2},X^{3},X^{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k<l}^{4}\Big\langle\phi(X^{k})\partial_{\mu_{k}}\phi(X^{k})^{*}\cdot\phi(X^{l})\partial_{\mu_{l}}\phi(X^{l})^{*}\cdot

(310)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\prod_{j\neq k,l}^{4}\phi(X^{j})^{*}\partial_{\mu_{j}}\phi(X^{j})\Big\rangle.

We note that
\tilde{\Pi}^{(0),{\rm I,II,III}}
contain respectively 12, 48 and 36 Wick contractions.

We set
(X^{3},\mu_{3}):=(z,\sigma)
and then
(X^{4},\mu_{4}):=(X^{3},\mu_{3})
, and write out the
z
-dependence explicitly,
since we want to integrate over
z
at a later stage. We use the group of permutations
{\cal S}_{n}
, which
contains
n!
elements. The result of the Wick contractions is

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}^{(0),{\rm I}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\partial_{\sigma}^{z}G(z-X^{\pi(3)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~

(311)

\displaystyle\qquad\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-z),

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}^{(0),{\rm II}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})=2\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\Big\{

(312)

\displaystyle~~\partial_{\sigma}^{z}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{z}G(z-X^{\pi(3)})~G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-z)

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\sigma}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-z)~G(z-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{(2)}-z)~\partial_{\sigma}^{z}G(z-X^{\pi(1)})\,\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-z)~\partial_{\sigma}^{z}G(z-X^{\pi(3)})\Big\},

as well as

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}^{(0),{\rm III}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})=2\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\Big\{

(313)

\displaystyle~~\partial_{\sigma}^{z}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{z}G(z-X^{\pi(3)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-z)

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{z}G(z-X^{\pi(3)})~\partial_{\sigma}^{X^{\pi(2)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-z)~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\sigma}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-z)~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})~G(z-X^{\pi(2)})\Big\}.

VII.4.2
One-tadpole contributions

Now to the contributions of the four-point function of
A_{\mu}(x)
involving exactly one tadpole,
coming from the term
\Delta{\cal L}=e^{2}\phi^{*}\phi A_{\mu}A_{\mu}
in the Lagrangian. Note that a factor
two appears because the term is quadratic in
A_{\mu}
.

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}(X^{1},X^{2},X^{3},X^{4})=-2\sum_{k<l}\delta_{\mu_{k}\mu_{l}}\delta(X^{k}-X^{l})\;\Big\langle\phi^{*}(X^{k})\phi(X^{k})\;\prod_{j\neq k,l}j_{\mu_{j}}(X^{j})\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k<l}\delta_{\mu_{k}\mu_{l}}\delta(X^{k}-X^{l})\;\Big\{\Big\langle\phi^{*}(X^{k})\phi(X^{k})\;\prod_{j\neq k,l}\phi(X^{j})^{*}\partial_{\mu_{j}}\phi(X^{j})\Big\rangle+\Big\langle\textrm{herm. conjug.}\Big\rangle

\displaystyle-\Big\langle\phi^{*}(X^{k})\phi(X^{k})\;\sum_{j\neq k,l}\phi(X^{j})^{*}\partial_{\mu_{j}}\phi(X^{j})\Big(\phi(X^{m})\partial_{\mu_{m}}\phi(X^{m})^{*}\Big)_{m=10-(j+k+l)}\Big\rangle\Big\}.

(314)

Disregarding the overall factor of two,
there are six permutations
(k<l)
and each gives rise to eight Wick contractions, yielding a total of 48 such contractions.
Now set
(X^{3},\mu_{3}):=(z,\sigma)
and
(X^{4},\mu_{4}):=(X^{3},\mu_{3})
.
There are three permutations in which
z
appears in the delta function, and three where it does not.

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})=2\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\Big\{\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(1)}}\delta(z-X^{\pi(1)})

(315)

\displaystyle\cdot\Big(\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})

\displaystyle+G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})\Big)

\displaystyle+\delta_{\mu_{\pi(1)}\mu_{\pi(2)}}\delta(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})

\displaystyle\cdot\Big(\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-z)~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~\partial_{\sigma}^{z}G(z-X^{\pi(1)})

\displaystyle+G(X^{\pi(1)}-z)~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\sigma}^{z}G(z-X^{\pi(3)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\sigma}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-z)~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~G(z-X^{\pi(1)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\sigma}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-z)~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})~G(z-X^{\pi(3)})\Big)\Big\}.

VII.4.3
Two-tadpole contributions

Finally, the contribution containing two tadpoles has the form

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}^{(2)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}(X^{1},X^{2},X^{3},X^{4})

(316)

\displaystyle=4\sum_{l=1}^{3}\delta_{\mu_{4}\mu_{l}}\,\delta(X^{4}-X^{l})\,\delta_{\mu_{j}\mu_{k}}\,\delta(X^{j}-X^{k})\Big\langle(\phi^{*}\phi)(X^{4})\,(\phi^{*}\phi)(X^{l})\Big\rangle,

where it is understood that
(j,k,l)
form a permutation of
(1,2,3)
. The expression can also be written as

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}^{(2)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}(X^{1},X^{2},X^{3},X^{4})

(317)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\delta_{\mu_{4}\mu_{\pi(1)}}\,\delta(X^{4}-X^{\pi(1)})\,\delta_{\mu_{\pi(2)}\mu_{\pi(3)}}\,\delta(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})\Big\langle(\phi^{*}\phi)(X^{4})\,(\phi^{*}\phi)(X^{\pi(2)})\Big\rangle,

Performing the contractions, one obtains

\displaystyle\tilde{\Pi}^{(2)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})

(318)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(1)}}\,\delta(z-X^{\pi(1)})\,\delta_{\mu_{\pi(2)}\mu_{\pi(3)}}\,\delta(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})^{2}.

VII.4.4
Test of the Ward identity

The Ward identity for current conservation reads

\partial_{\sigma}^{z}(\tilde{\Pi}^{(0)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}+\tilde{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}+\tilde{\Pi}^{(2)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}})(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})=0.

(319)

Taking into account the Green’s function property (
53
) of the scalar propagator
as well as the identity

\delta^{\prime}(z-x_{1})f(z)=\delta^{\prime}(z-x_{1})f(x_{1})-\delta(z-x_{1})f^{\prime}(x_{1})
,
a straightforward but tedious calculation yields

\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma}^{z}\tilde{\Pi}^{(0)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})=-2\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{z}\delta(z-X^{\pi(1)})\Big\{

(320)

\displaystyle~~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{2}}^{X^{2}}G(X^{2}-X^{3})~\partial_{\mu_{3}}^{X^{3}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})

\displaystyle+G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{2}}~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{3}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{2}}~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})\Big\}.

Similarly, using again Eq. (
53
), one finds

\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma}^{z}\tilde{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})=2\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\Big\{\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{z}\delta(z-X^{\pi(1)})

(321)

\displaystyle\cdot\Big(\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})

\displaystyle+G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})\Big)

\displaystyle-\delta_{\mu_{\pi(1)}\mu_{\pi(2)}}\delta(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})\;\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{z}\delta(X^{\pi(3)}-z)~G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})^{2}\Big\}.

Finally,

\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma}^{z}\tilde{\Pi}^{(2)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})=

(322)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{z}\,\delta(z-X^{\pi(1)})\,\delta_{\mu_{\pi(2)}\mu_{\pi(3)}}\,\delta(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})^{2}.

Thus one verifies that the Ward identity Eq. (
319
) is satisfied: the terms with two delta functions
cancel between
\tilde{\Pi}^{(1)}
and
\tilde{\Pi}^{(2)}
, while the terms with a single delta function
cancel between
\tilde{\Pi}^{(0)}
and
\tilde{\Pi}^{(1)}
.

VII.4.5
The expression for
i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\sigma}(X^{1},X^{2})

We recall the relation

i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\sigma}(X^{1},X^{2})=-\int d^{4}z\;z_{\rho}\;\tilde{\Pi}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})\Big|_{X^{3}=0},

(323)

and decompose the rank-five tensor according to

\displaystyle i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\sigma}(X^{1},X^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0,1,2}i\widehat{\Pi}^{(n)}_{\rho;\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\sigma}(X^{1},X^{2}),

(324)

\displaystyle i\widehat{\Pi}^{(n)}_{\rho;\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\sigma}(X^{1},X^{2})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-\int d^{4}z\;z_{\rho}\;\tilde{\Pi}^{(n)}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\sigma\mu_{3}}(X^{1},X^{2},z,X^{3})\Big|_{X^{3}=0}.

(325)

We will make use of the integral

\displaystyle H_{\rho}(X^{1},X^{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}z\,z_{\rho}\,G(z-X^{3})\,G(z-X^{1})

(326)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{m_{\pi}}{4\pi^{2}}\right)^{2}\Big[g_{\rho}(X^{3}-X^{1})+X^{1}_{\rho}\,p(|X^{3}-X^{1}|)\Big],

(327)

where

\displaystyle g_{\rho}(y)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int d^{4}z\;z_{\rho}\;\frac{K_{1}(m_{\pi}|y-z|)}{|y-z|}\;\frac{K_{1}(m_{\pi}|z|)}{|z|}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{m_{\pi}^{2}}y_{\rho}\,K_{0}(m_{\pi}|y|),

(328)

\displaystyle p(|y|)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int d^{4}z\;\frac{K_{1}(m_{\pi}|y-z|)}{|y-z|}\frac{K_{1}(m_{\pi}|z|)}{|z|}=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{m_{\pi}^{2}}\,K_{0}(m_{\pi}|y|).

(329)

Simplifying, one finds

H_{\rho}(X^{1},X^{3})=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}K_{0}(m_{\pi}|X^{3}-X^{1}|)\;(X^{1}_{\rho}+X^{3}_{\rho}).

(330)

What is needed in the following is the
antisymmetrized derivative

\partial_{[\sigma}^{X^{3}}H_{\rho]}(X^{1},X^{3})=-\frac{1}{4}G(X^{3}-X^{1})\,[X^{1}_{\rho},X^{3}_{\sigma}],

(331)

where we have introduced the notation

[X^{1}_{\rho},X^{3}_{\sigma}]\equiv X^{1}_{\rho}X^{3}_{\sigma}-X^{3}_{\rho}X^{1}_{\sigma}
.

A straightforward but lengthy calculation then leads to the expressions

\displaystyle i\widehat{\Pi}^{(0)}_{[\rho;\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\sigma]}(X^{1},X^{2})

(332)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\Big\{G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~[X^{\pi(1)}_{\rho},X^{\pi(3)}_{\sigma}]

\displaystyle+G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})(\delta_{\mu_{\pi(1)}\rho}\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(3)}}-\delta_{\rho\mu_{\pi(3)}}\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(1)}})

\displaystyle+G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~(\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(3)}}X^{\pi(1)}_{\rho}-\delta_{\rho\mu_{\pi(3)}}X^{\pi(1)}_{\sigma})

\displaystyle+G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~(\delta_{\rho\mu_{\pi(1)}}X^{\pi(3)}_{\sigma}-\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(1)}}X^{\pi(3)}_{\rho})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})~[X^{\pi(3)}_{\rho},X^{\pi(1)}_{\sigma}-X^{\pi(2)}_{\sigma}]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})(\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(1)}}X^{\pi(3)}_{\rho}-\delta_{\rho\mu_{\pi(1)}}X^{\pi(3)}_{\sigma})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})(\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(3)}}X^{\pi(2)}_{\rho}-\delta_{\rho\mu_{\pi(3)}}X^{\pi(2)}_{\sigma})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(1)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~[X^{\pi(1)}_{\rho},X^{\pi(2)}_{\sigma}-X^{\pi(3)}_{\sigma}]

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})(\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(1)}}X^{\pi(3)}_{\rho}-\delta_{\rho\mu_{\pi(1)}}X^{\pi(3)}_{\sigma})\Big\},

\displaystyle i\widehat{\Pi}^{(1)}_{[\rho;\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\sigma]}(X^{1},X^{2})+i\widehat{\Pi}^{(2)}_{[\rho;\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\sigma]}(X^{1},X^{2})=\sum_{\pi\in{\cal S}_{3}}\Big\{(\delta_{\rho\mu_{\pi(1)}}X^{\pi(1)}_{\sigma}-\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(1)}}X^{\pi(1)}_{\rho})\qquad

(333)

\displaystyle\cdot\Big(2G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(2)}}G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(1)}}G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(2)})~\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(1)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})

\displaystyle+\partial_{\mu_{\pi(3)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}\partial_{\mu_{\pi(2)}}^{X^{\pi(3)}}G(X^{\pi(3)}-X^{\pi(2)})~G(X^{\pi(1)}-X^{\pi(3)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})

\displaystyle+\delta_{\mu_{\pi(2)}\mu_{\pi(3)}}\delta(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle+\delta_{\mu_{\pi(2)}\mu_{\pi(3)}}\delta(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(3)})~G(X^{\pi(2)}-X^{\pi(1)})^{2}(\delta_{\sigma\mu_{\pi(1)}}X^{\pi(2)}_{\rho}-\delta_{\rho\mu_{\pi(1)}}X^{\pi(2)}_{\sigma})\Big\}.

The setting of
X^{3}
to zero is implied in the two equations above (see Eq. (
323
)).
In summary, the charged-pion-loop contribution to the function

i\widehat{\Pi}
is given by
Eqs. (
324
), (
332
) and (
333
).

A final step is required if one wants to perform numerical integrations over
i\widehat{\Pi}
in order
to obtain
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
, namely to isolate the delta-function-like contributions, which we also call
contact contributions. The last two terms of Eq. (
333
) are explicitly contact contributions.
Eq. (
332
), however, also contributes contact terms, because second derivatives of the scalar propagator appear.
Using the defining property (
53
) of the propagator, these second-derivative terms can be written as

\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}G(x)=\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}-4\frac{x_{\mu}x_{\nu}}{x^{2}}\right)\frac{G^{\prime}(x)}{|x|}+\frac{x_{\mu}x_{\nu}}{x^{2}}\,m_{\pi}^{2}\,G(x)-\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\mu\nu}\,\delta(x),

(334)

where
G^{\prime}(x)=\frac{d}{d|x|}G(x)=\frac{-m_{\pi}^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\frac{K_{2}(m_{\pi}|x|)}{|x|}
.
We have taken into account the fact that, applied to smooth test functions,

\left(\delta_{\mu\nu}-4\frac{x_{\mu}x_{\nu}}{x^{2}}\right)\delta(x)=0
,
so that
\frac{x_{\mu}x_{\nu}}{x^{2}}\delta(x)
can be substituted by
\frac{1}{4}\delta_{\mu\nu}\delta(x)
.
Collecting all the contributions proportional to a delta function
in
i\widehat{\Pi}_{[\rho;\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\sigma]}(X^{1},X^{2})
, we find in total

\displaystyle i\widehat{\Pi}_{[\rho;\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\sigma]}(X^{1},X^{2})_{\rm contact}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\Big[\delta_{\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\,\delta(X^{2})\,G(X^{1})^{2}\,(\delta_{\rho\mu_{1}}X^{1}_{\sigma}-\delta_{\sigma\mu_{1}}X^{1}_{\rho})

(335)

\displaystyle-\delta_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\,\delta(X^{1}-X^{2})\,G(X^{2})^{2}\,(\delta_{\rho\mu_{3}}X^{2}_{\sigma}-\delta_{\sigma\mu_{3}}X^{2}_{\rho})

\displaystyle+\delta_{\mu_{1}\mu_{3}}\,\delta(X^{1})\,G(X^{2})^{2}\,(\delta_{\rho\mu_{2}}X^{2}_{\sigma}-\delta_{\sigma\mu_{2}}X^{2}_{\rho})\Big].

We note that these terms are integrable; for instance, the first term goes like
1/|X^{1}|^{3}
for small
|X^{1}|
.

VIII
Applications and tests of the QED kernel

In this section, we combine the QED kernel as computed in
section
V
with the four-point functions

i\widehat{\Pi}
given explicitly in section
VII
in order
to check whether the coordinate-space method developed here reproduces
known results. Furthermore, the method is tested for the case that the
QED fermion loop is computed on the lattice, reproducing the known
result after taking the continuum limit. Finally, in subsection

VIII.4
an overview of results obtained for the
fully connected part of the lattice QCD four-point function is presented.

VIII.1
Improved kernels

The master formula, given by Eq. (
29
), can be written in a slightly different way to optimize the lattice QCD calculation. First, as already noted in Ref.
Blum:2017cer
, the QED weight function is not uniquely defined. This freedom can be used to obtained a better behaved integrand with smaller statistical and systematic uncertainties. As shown below, it turns out to be a crucial ingredient for practical lattice QCD calculations. The idea is to remove large fluctuations or large cancellations in the integrand that do not affect the central value in the continuum and infinite volume but increase the statistical error and/or systematic effects in the estimator.
Second, a naive implementation of the master formula in lattice QCD calculations is rather expensive.
But the numerical cost can be considerably reduced by using a different formula, equivalent in the infinite volume limit. These two improvements are discussed next.

In the continuum and in infinite volume, the conservation of the electromagnetic current implies Eq. (
245
),
namely that the integral of the four-point function
i\widehat{\Pi}_{\rho;\mu\nu\lambda\sigma}(x,y)
over
x
without any

x
-dependent weight-factor vanishes. The same observation applies to the integral over the coordinate-vector
y
.
Therefore, in infinite volume, any function which depends only on
x
or
y
can be added to the QED kernel without affecting the final result. On the infinite lattice, and with Wilson fermions, this property still holds at finite lattice spacing if one uses the conserved vector current, see Eq. (
345b
). When using local vector currents, the result holds once the continuum limit has been taken.
We will consider four kernels
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
which differ only by such subtractions,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(0)}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)\,,

(336a)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,x)-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(y,y)\,,

(336b)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)-\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(0,y)-\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,0)\,,

(336c)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(3)}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)-\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,x)+\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(0,x)-\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(0,y)\,.

(336d)

In addition to vanishing when both arguments vanish,

\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(0,0)=0,\qquad(n=0,1,2,3),

(337)

(see Eq. (
39
) for the non-trivial cases
n=0
and
n=2
),
the subtracted kernels vanish in various special configurations,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}(x,x)=0\,,\quad\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}(x,0)=\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}(0,y)=0\,,\quad\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(3)}(x,0)=\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(3)}(x,x)=0\,.

(338)

In
Asmussen:2019act
;
Chao:2020kwq
we introduced a kernel tuneable by an arbitrary parameter
\Lambda
that approaches
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(0)}
when
\Lambda\rightarrow\infty
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}
when
\Lambda\rightarrow 0
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(\Lambda)}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle-\partial_{\mu}^{(x)}(x_{\alpha}e^{-\Lambda m^{2}x^{2}/2})\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma];\alpha\nu\lambda}(0,y)-\partial_{\nu}^{(y)}(y_{\alpha}e^{-\Lambda m^{2}y^{2}/2})\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{[\rho,\sigma];\mu\alpha\lambda}(x,0),

(339)

as we empirically found that with
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(3)}
, the integrand was too long-ranged, while with
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(0)}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}
it was too peaked at short distances. In our most recent works
Chao:2021tvp
;
Chao:2022xzg
, we presented results exclusively with
\Lambda=0.4
.

Figure 6:
Wick contractions for the connected contribution in Method 1.

For each quark flavour
f
,
the quark-connected part of the hadronic four-point function
\Pi
involves three different Wick contractions.
Each of those appears twice, with opposite fermion-number flow, resulting in a purely real contribution; see Eq. (
265
)
and Fig. ​
6
.
Computing explicitly all three contractions, and using the master formula given by Eq. (
29
), amounts to what we call Method 1
Chao:2020kwq
. To estimate all three contractions, we first compute point-to-all propagators with sources located at the origin and at the site
y
. Then, we perform sequential inversions using the propagators, summed over
z
and with the weight factor
z_{\rho}
, as sequential sources to finally contract both results and summing over
x
. Since one needs to anti-symmetrize between
\rho
and
\sigma
, it amounts to 6 sequential inversions for each primary inversion.

Alternatively, we could choose the first Wick contraction,
I^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,w)
of Eq. (
266
) corresponding
to the leftmost diagram in Fig.
6
,
as a reference and swap the vertices at the level of the muon line – an idea that was already exploited in
Blum:2015gfa
.
Defining the gauge-field average of the three Wick contractions as follows,

\widetilde{\Pi}^{(j)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z)\equiv\langle-2\,{\rm Re}\;I^{(j)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z,0)\rangle,\qquad(j=1,2,3),

(340)

we obtain the Method 2
Chao:2020kwq
estimator (
A=0,1,2,3
or
\Lambda
)

a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}=-\frac{me^{6}}{3}\int{d}^{4}y\int{d}^{4}x\,\\
\Big\{\,\Big(\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(A)}_{[\rho,\sigma],\mu\nu\lambda}(x,y)+\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(A)}_{[\rho,\sigma],\nu\mu\lambda}(y,x)-\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(A)}_{[\rho,\sigma],\lambda\nu\mu}(x,x-y)\Big)\,\int d^{4}z\,z_{\rho}\;\widetilde{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z)\\
+\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(A)}_{[\rho,\sigma],\lambda\nu\mu}(x,x-y)\,x_{\rho}\,\int{d}^{4}z\,\widetilde{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z)\Big\}\,.

(341)

For the quark-connected contribution, and with
A\doteq\Lambda
, this equation is the starting point for all our lattice-QCD results
in Refs.
Chao:2021tvp
;
Chao:2022xzg
, as well as the final results in Ref.
Chao:2020kwq
.
The advantage of this representation is that
all propagators can be expressed in terms of the two point-to-all propagators with sources located at the origin and on site
y

by exploiting the
\gamma_{5}
-hermiticity relation (
264
).
Eq. (
341
) can be proven starting from the master formula (
29
) and using the identities

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pi}^{(2)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\nu\mu\sigma\lambda}(y,x,z)\,,

(342a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pi}^{(3)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\lambda\nu\sigma\mu}(-x,y-x,z-x)\,

(342b)

as well as Eq. (
38
).
In practice, we reduce the master formulae to a one-dimensional
integral over the variable
|y|
; the integrand then differs between Method 1 and Method 2, even in the continuum limit. The advantage of Method 2 is that only one additional propagator needs to be computed for each value of
y
: for
N
values of
|y|
, the number of quark propagators that need to be computed is
N+1
compared to
7(N+1)
for Method 1, where sequential inversions are used.
In addition, combining all possible pairs of quark propagators allows one to compute O
(N^{2})
independent data, which may include multiple statistical samples of the same
|y|
.

In general, the last term in Eq. (
341
) does not vanish, in spite of Eq. (
239
) holding,
because
\Pi^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z)
is only one of three contributing Wick contractions
to the four-point function. In fact, as a Ward identity following from current conservation, one can show that

\int d^{4}z\;\widetilde{\Pi}^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z)=(-2y_{\sigma})\;\Big\langle{\rm Re}\,{\rm Tr}\{\gamma_{\mu}S(x,y)\gamma_{\nu}S(y,0)\gamma_{\lambda}S(0,x)\}\Big\rangle.

(343)

However, for a pseudoscalar-pole contribution, this term vanishes,

\displaystyle\int{d}^{4}z\,\Pi^{(1);\pi^{0}}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z)=0\,,

(344)

because
12
12

12

As noted in
Asmussen:2019act
;
Chao:2021tvp
, the quark-level Wick contraction

\Pi^{(1)}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z)
does not contain
the diagram in which the
\pi^{0}
propagates between the pair
(0,y)
and the pair

(x,z)
of vertices, which corresponds to the third (and last) term in Eq. (
248
).
The first two terms, which do contribute, vanish at
q_{3}=0
, leading to the conclusion (
344
).
the Fourier-transform of

\Pi^{(1);\pi^{0}}_{\mu\nu\sigma\lambda}(x,y,z)
contains an explicit
factor of
q_{3}
, the momentum dual to
z
. As a consequence, we expect
smaller finite-size effect on this term.

In summary, Method 2 is numerically far cheaper to apply. It leads to
a mild broadening of the overall integrand in
|y|
(see Fig.
7
,
as well as Figs. 18 and 19 of Ref.
Chao:2020kwq
), which a suitable tuning of the parameter
\Lambda

can counteract. Thus the combination of Method 2 and the kernel
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(\Lambda)}

was invaluable to our lattice QCD results in Refs.
Chao:2020kwq
;
Chao:2021tvp
;
Chao:2022xzg
.

VIII.2
Tests in the continuum

As a first check of our master formula, and to gain some insight on the shape of the integrand, we compute several contributions to
a_{\mu}^{\rm LbL}
using the expressions for
\widehat{\Pi}
derived in the previous section, and compare them with the known results obtained in momentum space.

VIII.2.1
The pion-pole contribution to
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}

Figure 7:
The integrand with respect to the variable
|y|

leading to
a_{\mu}^{\rm HLbL}
for the neutral pion-pole with a VMD
form factor and a pion mass of 135 MeV.
The integrals over
|x|
and
\cos\beta
have already been performed at this stage.
The different curves correspond to the kernels introduced in Eq. (
336
) and Eq. (
VIII.1
). Left: Method 1. Right: Method 2. Note that for method 2, the kernel
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}
and
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(3)}
yield identical curves.

The pion-pole contribution is estimated using the expression of
\widehat{\Pi}
derived in Sec.
VII.2
, assuming a VMD transition form factor. We note that, when using Method 2, one needs to find the correct mapping between the three contractions in momentum space (see Eq. (
248
)) and the reference contraction in position space. This can be done using partially-quenched chiral perturbation theory and we refer the reader to Ref
Chao:2021tvp
for more details.
After integration over
|x|
and
\cos\beta
, the integrand, as a function of
|y|
, is displayed in Fig.
7
for both Methods 1 and 2 and all four kernels
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(n)}
with a pion mass of
m_{\pi}=135
MeV.
For pion masses in the range [135 - 600] MeV, we reproduce the results, obtained from the three-dimensional integral representation in momentum space in Ref.
Jegerlehner:2009ry
, at the percent level. The results are summarized in Table
2
.
Using the standard kernel, one observes that this contribution is remarkably long-range with a negative tail at large
|y|
. In particular, one needs a very large lattice
L\gg 5~
fm to capture the negative tail at the physical pion mass. When using the method 1, the integrands corresponding to the kernel 2 and 3 are less peaked at short distances, approach zero faster at long distances and remain positive. When using Method 2, the integrand for both kernels 2 and 3 are identical
but also more long range. Since this setup is considerably cheaper for practical lattice QCD calculations, one can attempt to correct for finite-size effects on this contribution by computing the pion-transition form factor on the same set of ensembles, as done in Ref.
Gerardin:2019vio
. See Ref.
Chao:2021tvp
for a practical implementation.

The lepton-loop contribution to
a_{\mu}^{\rm LbL}

The lepton-loop contribution to

is estimated using
the expression of
\widehat{\Pi}
derived in
Sec.

with

where

m_{l}

summarized in Table
3
and the shape of the integrand,
as a function of
|y|
, is shown in Fig.
8
. For

m_{l}=m,2m
we reproduce the analytically known results
for
a_{\mu}^{\rm LbL}
in QED
Laporta:1992pa
;
Passera_private_communication
with a precision of about 1%. As can
be seen from the plots, the integrand is quite steep close to the
origin and we probe the QED kernel at short distances. We observe that
the height of the peak grows for smaller masses of the lepton in the
loop. For
m_{l}=m/2
this rise to the peak is very steep,
and we observe a 2.3% deviation from the exact result.
It thus appears difficult to obtain
a_{\mu}^{\rm LbL}
at the percent level
with our implementation of the QED kernel for such a long-range contribution.
As for the pion, the integrand resulting from the standard kernel also exhibits a long
negative tail. Again, the kernel 2 and 3 are peaked at short ranges when
using Method 1, a feature that does not hold when switching to Method 2.

) and

), (

.

VIII.3
The lepton-loop on the lattice

We will now show the results of performing a full lattice calculation of the lepton loop contribution using both Methods 1 and 2. This was a first step towards the full lattice QCD calculations, and an important benchmark of our implementation of the position-space approach.

The correlation function is computed on a
L^{4}
lattice using unit gauge links, (anti-)periodic boundary conditions in (time-)space and Wilson fermions.
For a fixed vertex position
y
, the sums over the sites
x
and
z
in Eq. (
29
)
are performed explicitly.
After which we have a one-dimensional integral that can be sampled sufficiently finely using the variable
|y|
, for all values of
y=(n,n,n,n)
with
0<n<L/2
. We also consider both the local (
l
) and the conserved
(c)
vector currents, which have different discretization effects,

\displaystyle j_{\mu}^{(l)}(x)

\displaystyle=\overline{\psi}(x)\gamma_{\mu}\mathcal{Q}\psi_{j}(x)\,,

(345a)

\displaystyle j_{\mu}^{(c)}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\overline{\psi}(x+a\hat{\mu})(1+\gamma_{\mu})U^{{\dagger}}_{\mu}(x)\mathcal{Q}\psi(x)-\overline{\psi}(x)(1-\gamma_{\mu})U_{\mu}(x)\mathcal{Q}\psi(x+a\hat{\mu})\right)\,.

(345b)

Here,
\mathcal{Q}
is the lepton charge in units of
e
.
Let
m_{0}
be the bare subtracted lepton mass, related to the hopping parameter
\kappa_{\ell}
via

am_{0}=(1/\kappa_{\ell}-1/\kappa_{\rm cr})/2
with
\kappa_{\rm cr}=1/8
.
In the free theory, the local vector current has the advantage of being automatically O
(a)
-improved, if one uses as multiplicative renormalization factor
(1+b_{\rm V}am_{0})
with
b_{\rm V}=1

Sint:1997jx
. While no multiplicative renormalization of the conserved vector current is needed, an additive improvement term with a coefficient
c_{\rm V}=1/2
is required to remove ‘on-shell’ O(
a
) lattice artifacts, but not included here (see e.g. ​
Gerardin:2018kpy
). Indeed, for both current discretizations, additional lattice artifacts scaling linearly with the lattice spacing are expected to arise from the region where two or more currents are separated by a distance on the order of the lattice spacing. Our line of constant physics is defined by a constant renormalized mass of the lepton in the loop,
m_{\ell}=\;
cst. Including O
(a)
effects, one has
m_{\ell}=Z_{\rm m}\,m_{0}(1+b_{\rm m}am_{0})
, with
Z_{\rm m}=1
and
b_{\rm m}=-1/2

Sint:1997jx
, so that the bare subtracted quark mass has to be adjusted in the simulation. Finally, we are working with the QCD code and the result must be divided by
N_{c}=3
, the number of colors.

We have used seven lattices, with the same physical volume
L\,m_{l}=7.2
, and the continuum extrapolation is performed assuming the simple functional form

a_{\mu}^{\rm LbL}(a)=a_{\mu}^{\rm LbL}(0)+\alpha\,a+\beta\,a^{2}\,,

(346)

where only the four lattices with the smallest lattice spacings are
included in the fit. The result using the first strategy with
m_{l}=2m_{\mu}
is shown in Fig.
10
for the kernel

\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(0)}
(left panel) and for the subtracted kernel
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}

(right panel);

Note the very different ranges on the
y
-axis. In both
cases, we use four different discretizations of the correlation
function (all combinations of local and conserved vector currents at
sites
x
and
z
). The continuum extrapolation, at fixed volume, is
given by the dashed lines. To estimate the
correction due to finite-size effects, a new set of two lattices, with larger volumes, are
used. The finite-size effect correction is assumed to be independent
of the lattice spacing and is estimated as the difference between the
small and the large volumes at a given lattice spacing. The corrected
results are finally given by the plain lines. One observes much
smaller discretization effects for the kernel
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}
in the right
panel where one only has to extrapolate
a_{\mu}^{\rm LbL}\times 10^{11}
from about 100 to 140, compared to rather large
extrapolations with kernel
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(0)}
in the left panel (even from
negative values). The results of the continuum extrapolations are collected in table
4
.
The same observation applies to the following two
figures.
We also note that very similar results are obtained with the
kernel
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{(3)}
.

(right panel) using Method 1. We use

. The colors correspond to different discretizations of the correlation function.
